## Тест academic-3

### Секция 1: Seeing with Sound

A. On a warm summer evening, the small dark shapes that dart and swerve above a garden pond are doing something remarkable. Many of them are catching insects no bigger than a grain of rice, in light so poor that a human would struggle to see a hand in front of their face. For centuries, nobody could explain how they managed it. The answer, as we now know, is that most bats find their way by listening to the echoes of their own voices, a skill known as echolocation. They are not alone in this: dolphins and other toothed whales use the same principle underwater, and a handful of cave-dwelling birds rely on a simpler version.

B. The first serious attempt to solve the mystery took place in the 1790s, when an Italian naturalist released bats whose eyes had been covered into a darkened room crossed by fine threads. Remarkably, the animals flew between the threads without touching them. Soon afterwards, a Swiss surgeon showed that bats whose ears had been plugged with wax collided with obstacles and flew reluctantly. Hearing was clearly involved, yet the bats appeared to be silent, and many scholars found it impossible to accept that an animal could navigate by sounds that no one could detect. A rival theory, that bats possessed an extraordinarily sensitive sense of touch in their wings, became widely accepted, and the question remained essentially unanswered for well over a century. Only in the late 1930s did researchers, using a newly built microphone capable of picking up very high frequencies, demonstrate that flying bats produce a steady stream of calls pitched far above the limit of human hearing.

C. How does the system actually work? A hunting bat emits short pulses of sound, usually at frequencies between about 20 and 120 kilohertz; most adults cannot hear anything above roughly 20 kilohertz. Some species call through an open mouth, while others send sound out through their nostrils, which are often surrounded by folds of skin called nose-leaves that help to direct the beam. The calls are surprisingly powerful, in some species as intense at close range as a smoke alarm. To avoid deafening itself, a bat tightens tiny muscles in its middle ear at the moment it calls and relaxes them a fraction of a second later, just in time to catch the faint returning echo. The delay between call and echo tells the animal how far away an object is, while slight differences in the timing and loudness of the echo at each ear reveal its direction.

D. Bats also change the way they call as a hunt unfolds. While searching, a bat may produce around ten calls a second. Once it detects an insect, the calls become shorter and more frequent, until, in the final moments before capture, they can reach about two hundred a second, a rapid sequence that researchers call the 'feeding buzz'. The choice of pitch involves a trade-off. High-frequency sound has a short wavelength and so bounces back well even from tiny prey, but it fades quickly in air. For this reason, species that hunt in open sky tend to use lower calls, which travel further, whereas those that pick small prey from leaves in cluttered forest rely on higher ones.

E. Not every bat echolocates. Most of the fruit bats of Africa, Asia and Australia find their food using large eyes and a keen sense of smell, and only a few of them produce simple clicks, made with the tongue rather than the voice box. Meanwhile, the insects that bats eat have not stood still. Some moths have evolved ears that are sensitive to bat calls, and when they detect an approaching hunter they fold their wings and drop, or fly in erratic loops. Certain tiger moths go further, answering with ultrasonic clicks of their own that may warn the bat of their bad taste or confuse its sonar.

F. Human technology has followed a similar path, though largely by independent invention: naval sonar was developed in the early twentieth century with little reference to bats. The ultrasonic sensors that warn drivers when they are reversing towards a wall work on the same basic principle. More recently, however, engineers have begun to copy specific features of bat behaviour. Gareth Mwangi, an engineer working on small indoor drones, programmed one of his aircraft to pulse slowly in open space and to increase its rate sharply as it approached an obstacle, much as a hunting bat does. In his trials, the drone used noticeably less battery power than an identical model pulsing rapidly all the time, without hitting more obstacles. In another project, Keiko Arashiro shaped the sound emitter of a handheld guide for blind pedestrians to resemble a bat's nose-leaf; in a small trial, she reported, users detected thin poles more reliably than with a conventional device.

G. Even so, the machines remain crude by comparison. A bat leaving a crowded cave can pick out its own echoes from the calls of thousands of neighbours, a feat that engineers cannot yet reproduce and that biologists do not fully understand. After tens of millions of years of refinement, the bat's sonar still has a great deal to teach its imitators.

### Секция 2: Paying to Drive into Town

A. On a weekday morning in almost any large city, the main roads into the centre fill up long before the shops open. Each additional car slows every other vehicle a little, yet the driver who joins the queue pays nothing for the time that everyone else loses. Economists have long described this as a hidden cost: because road space is free at the point of use, it is consumed far more heavily than it would be if drivers had to account for the delay they impose. If a charge is attached to entering the most crowded areas at the busiest times, the argument runs, some trips will be moved to another hour, switched to buses or trains, or abandoned altogether, and the remaining traffic should flow more freely.

B. For decades the idea remained largely theoretical, partly because collecting money from thousands of moving vehicles seemed impractical. Singapore was the first city to put it into practice, in 1975. Motorists wishing to enter the central business district during the morning peak had to buy a paper licence in advance and display it on the windscreen, while officers stationed at entry points checked each car as it passed. The crude system worked: traffic in the zone dropped sharply. In 1998 the paper licences gave way to an electronic system that deducts the fee automatically from a card inside the vehicle, allowing prices to vary by location and time of day.

C. Most later schemes rely instead on cameras. The city first draws a boundary, often following a ring road, around the area it wishes to protect. Cameras are then installed at every point where a vehicle can cross that line, and each one photographs the number plates of passing traffic. Software converts the images into registration numbers, which are compared with a database of drivers who have paid for that day. Anyone whose plate does not appear by the deadline is traced through the national vehicle register, and a penalty notice is sent to the owner by post. Ambulances and buses are normally exempt, and residents who live inside the zone may receive a large discount.

D. London adopted a camera-based charge in 2003. In the early years, the number of vehicles entering the zone fell noticeably and buses ran more reliably. The gain in journey speeds, however, did not last. Over the following decade, space on many central streets was handed over to wider pavements, bus lanes and cycle routes, and a growing volume of roadworks and delivery vans took up much of the remainder. Critics pointed to the slower traffic as proof of failure; supporters replied that without the charge the roads would have been far more crowded still. That claim is difficult to test, because no one can observe the city as it would otherwise have been.

E. Stockholm offers a different lesson, about public opinion. Before the Swedish capital ran a seven-month trial in 2006, polls showed most residents opposed to the idea. Once the charge was in place and queues on the bridges into the centre shrank, attitudes softened, and in a referendum held after the trial a majority of city voters backed keeping it. The charge became permanent in 2007. Farida Kassab, a transport economist, studied this shift by interviewing the same group of commuters before and after the trial. She found that many former opponents had not changed their daily routes at all; what had changed was that the charge now felt like an ordinary part of city life. In her view, people tend to resist almost any change to how roads are used, and that resistance fades once the new arrangement becomes familiar.

F. Not everyone is persuaded. The most frequent objection concerns fairness. A flat daily fee is a trivial sum for a well-paid executive but a real burden for a care worker on a low wage whose shift begins before the first trains run. Shopkeepers raise a second concern. Callum Brodie, who represents independent retailers in a mid-sized city considering a charge, argues that many customers will simply drive to out-of-town shopping centres, where parking is free, rather than pay to reach the high street, and he expects smaller shops to suffer first. A third worry is that traffic does not disappear but moves to the streets just outside the boundary, whose residents suffer the extra noise and fumes without sharing any of the benefits. Finally, some people object in principle to cameras recording every journey into the centre.

G. Supporters accept that these objections deserve serious answers, and much depends on what happens to the money raised. Spending the revenue on cheaper and more frequent buses, and offering discounts to essential workers, can meet the fairness complaint directly; lower-income travellers, after all, are more likely to use public transport in the first place. Careful monitoring of the streets near the boundary can show whether traffic is really being pushed elsewhere. The broader lesson from the cities that have tried charging is that it works best as one part of a wider transport plan, not as a stand-alone cure for congestion.

### Секция 3: Two Things at Once?

A. Ask almost anyone to describe a typical working day and you will hear about juggling: answering messages during meetings, listening to podcasts while drafting reports, switching between a dozen open windows. Job advertisements ask for candidates who can multitask, as though it were an ordinary skill like typing. My argument in this article is that, where both activities genuinely require thought, this ability does not exist in the form most people imagine. What looks like doing two things at once is almost always doing one thing and then the other in quick succession, and each switch costs more than we notice. Several researchers disagree with me, some strongly, and their objections deserve a fair hearing.

B. The basic evidence is not new. Researchers have long found that when people must respond to two signals presented very close together in time, their response to the second is delayed, as if it has to wait for the first to pass through some kind of mental bottleneck. Studies of task switching show a similar pattern: moving back and forth between two activities takes longer, and produces more errors, than finishing one before starting the other. Each change requires the mind to set aside one set of rules and retrieve another, and that adjustment is not free. A single cost may amount to only a fraction of a second, but across a day of constant interruptions the losses accumulate. Crucially, people tend to underestimate them, because the feeling of being busy is easily confused with the feeling of being productive.

C. None of this means that humans can do only one thing at a time in every sense. We walk while chatting, and an experienced driver can hold a conversation on an empty motorway. The difference is that one of these activities has become so well practised that it demands little conscious attention. Yet the arrangement is fragile. When the road ahead suddenly becomes complicated, the conversation tends to stop, often without the driver noticing that it has. That pause is the bottleneck reasserting itself. Talking while driving is therefore not evidence against my position but an illustration of its limits.

D. The most serious challenge comes from Elif Strandberg, a cognitive scientist who has spent a decade studying conference interpreters. Simultaneous interpreters listen to a speaker in one language while producing a translation in another, often only a few words behind. Strandberg argues that this is plainly two demanding tasks performed together, and she regards the 'myth' label as an insult to a profession built on exactly this ability. I admire interpreters enormously, but I do not think their case refutes mine. They train for years, and much of that training appears to turn parts of the work, such as common phrases and familiar sentence patterns, into routines that no longer require deliberate effort. Interpreters also typically work in pairs, handing over to a colleague every half hour or so, because the strain soon becomes unsustainable. An ability that must be rationed so carefully looks less like everyday multitasking than like an exceptional, hard-won skill.

E. Rohan Valdivia raises a different objection. In a laboratory study, he asked volunteers to alternate between two sorting tasks every day for six weeks. By the end, the delay caused by each switch had shrunk to roughly a third of its original size. Valdivia concludes that switching costs reflect inexperience rather than a fixed feature of the mind, and that calling multitasking a myth discourages people from developing it. His results are interesting, but his volunteers repeated the same two tasks thousands of times. Office life rarely offers such predictability: the message that interrupts a report is different every time. Moreover, even after six weeks of practice, the cost had shrunk rather than vanished.

F. Other findings support my position more directly. Mireille Kowalczyk asked more than two hundred office workers to rate their own ability to handle several tasks at once, and then measured their performance on a series of switching exercises. She found no connection between how skilled people believed they were and how well they actually did. Kowalczyk is careful to point out that all her participants came from a single company, but the result fits a wider pattern: confidence in multitasking seems to rest on the sensation of activity rather than on measured results.

G. Finally, the neuroscientist Kwame Iturbe objects to the whole debate. The brain, he points out, is always running many processes in parallel: maintaining balance, regulating breathing, making sense of what the eyes see. To declare multitasking a myth is, in his view, scientifically careless. He is right about the brain, and I accept that my claim concerns deliberate, effortful tasks rather than everything the nervous system does. But I do not accept that the disagreement is merely a matter of words. When managers expect staff to write a proposal while monitoring a constant stream of messages, they are not thinking about breathing. They are assuming a capacity that the evidence suggests most of us lack.

H. What follows in practice? Not, I think, that individuals should feel guilty every time they glance at a phone. The more useful lesson concerns design: offices, schedules and software could be arranged so that demanding work is protected from interruption for sensible stretches of time. That would require employers to stop treating constant availability as a virtue, which is a harder change to make than any personal habit, but a far more valuable one.

## Тест academic-4

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2567. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":7,"high":7}.

### Секция 1: The Box That Changed Trade

A. For most of recorded history, loading a ship looked much the same. Goods arrived at the quayside in sacks, barrels, bales and crates of every shape, and gangs of dockworkers carried, rolled or hoisted each item aboard, fitting it into the hold by hand. A single cargo ship in the early 1950s might hold tens of thousands of separate items, and loading or unloading it could take a week or more. Vessels often spent as many days tied up in port as they did at sea. The process was also costly in other ways: cargo was damaged by careless handling, and a share of it routinely disappeared into workers' pockets or onto passing lorries. For many goods, the expense of moving them across a few hundred metres of dock was a large fraction of the cost of the entire journey.

B. The notion of packing cargo into large, reusable boxes was not new. Railways in several countries had experimented with removable freight boxes in the early twentieth century, and some shipping lines used small steel crates, but these schemes stayed local and never spread widely. The decisive step came in 1956, when an American entrepreneur who had built his fortune running a trucking business bought a shipping company and had an old tanker converted to carry 58 detachable trailer bodies. It sailed from Newark to Houston, and the cost of loading it was a small fraction of the usual rate. What made the experiment important was not the box itself but the thinking behind it: the entrepreneur saw that the real business was not operating ships but moving goods, and that the ship was simply one link in a chain that also included lorries, trains and warehouses.

C. For the idea to work on a global scale, however, the boxes had to be identical. In the early years each company built containers to its own design, in lengths that varied from firm to firm, and a box belonging to one firm could not be lifted by another firm's cranes or fitted into its ships. Through the 1960s, engineers, manufacturers and government officials negotiated a common standard, and the International Organization for Standardization eventually settled on lengths of 20 and 40 feet, together with a uniform width and a strong steel fitting at each corner. These corner fittings, locked together with a simple twisting device, allow containers to be stacked many layers high and fastened securely to ships, rail wagons and lorry trailers. Standardisation also produced a new unit of measurement, the twenty-foot equivalent unit, or TEU. Above all, it meant that goods could be sealed inside a box at a factory and not handled again until they reached their final destination.

D. The new system transformed the geography of ports. A container terminal needs deep water for large ships, giant cranes and, above all, vast areas of flat land on which thousands of boxes can be stacked and sorted. Old harbours in the hearts of cities, squeezed between narrow streets and ageing warehouses, had no room to expand, and trade moved to new sites. In New York, activity drained away from the piers of Manhattan and Brooklyn towards spacious terminals across the harbour in New Jersey; in Britain, London's historic docks closed one after another while the small port of Felixstowe grew into the country's busiest container port. The human cost was severe. The economic historian Wilhelmina Oduya, in her study of three European ports, found that dock employment fell by roughly two-thirds within fifteen years of each port opening its first container terminal.

E. Ships changed just as dramatically. The first purpose-built container ships carried a few hundred boxes; the largest vessels today carry more than 20,000 TEU. Because cranes can move a container every minute or two, a ship that once spent a week in port may now be turned around in a matter of hours. Lower costs per box encouraged shipping lines to build ever bigger vessels and to merge into a handful of giant companies. Yet the port economist Kenji Varela argues that the most important gain was not cheapness but predictability. When manufacturers could be confident that a consignment would arrive on a given day, he says, they could reduce the stocks they held in reserve and spread production across several countries, assembling products from parts made thousands of kilometres apart.

F. Economists still debate how much of the growth in world trade since the 1960s can be credited to the container, but some have argued that its effect was greater than that of many trade agreements. Its success has also created new weaknesses. Because global supply chains depend on boxes arriving exactly when expected, a single blocked canal or a shortage of empty containers can delay deliveries around the world for months. And because a sealed steel box looks the same whatever it holds, customs authorities can physically inspect only a small proportion of those passing through their ports, which makes the system attractive to smugglers. The plain metal container remains one of the least noticed inventions of the twentieth century, but few objects have done more to shape the world economy.

### Секция 2: Can We Rebuild the Reef?

A. Coral reefs occupy less than one percent of the sea floor, yet they shelter roughly a quarter of all known marine species, protect coastlines from waves and support fisheries and tourism on which hundreds of millions of people depend. Over recent decades, however, many reefs have lost a large share of their living coral, and in some regions the reef that divers see today is a grey, weed-covered skeleton of what existed a generation ago. Against this background, a growing number of projects aim not merely to protect what remains but to put coral back.

B. To understand why reefs are struggling, it helps to know that a coral is not a plant or a rock but a colony of small animals called polyps. Inside their tissues live microscopic algae which, through photosynthesis, supply the coral with most of its food and give it its colour. This partnership is sensitive to temperature. When the water stays even one or two degrees above its usual summer maximum for several weeks, the relationship breaks down and the coral expels its algae, turning white. Such bleached corals are not yet dead, and they can recover if conditions return to normal quickly; if the heat persists, however, they starve. Some species cope better than others, but few escape entirely. As oceans have warmed, mass bleaching events that were once rare have become far more frequent, leaving less time for recovery between them.

C. Heat is not the only problem. As seawater absorbs carbon dioxide from the atmosphere, it becomes more acidic, which makes it harder for corals to build their limestone skeletons. Closer to shore, soil and fertiliser washed off farmland cloud the water and encourage the growth of seaweed, while overfishing removes the grazing fish that would normally keep that seaweed in check. Outbreaks of disease, which appear to spread more easily among stressed colonies, add a further threat. A reef weakened by these local pressures is often less able to survive a heatwave.

D. The most common restoration approach is sometimes called coral gardening. Small pieces are snipped from healthy colonies and hung on underwater frames or ropes, where they grow, protected from predators, until they are large enough to be fixed onto a damaged reef. A refinement of this technique involves cutting certain slow-growing corals into tiny fragments; because the wounded edges heal rapidly, these pieces can spread far faster than an intact colony. Other teams collect the eggs and sperm that corals release during annual spawning events, raise the larvae in tanks and then release them over degraded areas, an approach that can, in principle, cover much larger areas. Most nurseries are run by small organisations that rely heavily on volunteer divers.

E. How well does any of this work? A long-term survey led by marine ecologist Talia Gunawardena followed transplanted corals at forty sites for five years. Her team found that the method used mattered less than the place chosen: where the water was clear and herbivorous fish were plentiful, most transplants survived, whereas at sites polluted by run-off, the majority had died by the third year. In Gunawardena's view, restoration cannot succeed unless the local causes of decline are dealt with first. Fisheries researcher Sione Lautaha reached a related conclusion in a village-run project in the Pacific. Survival improved markedly, he reported, once local fishers helped choose sites, because they knew which spots were sheltered from strong currents. He also noted that the villagers who tended the nurseries became more willing to respect fishing restrictions nearby.

F. A more ambitious idea is to give corals a better chance against warming itself. Geneticist Beatriz Quintanilla has crossed corals from naturally hot lagoons with those from cooler waters, and in her laboratory trials some offspring withstood temperatures that bleached their cooler-water parents. She warns, though, that these heat-tolerant corals grew noticeably more slowly, and that nobody yet knows whether such traits will remain useful if seas continue to warm. Her next step is to test the crossbred corals on an open reef, where conditions are far less predictable than in a tank.

G. Cost and scale remain the hardest obstacles. Economist Lars Edvardsen estimated that gardening projects typically spend tens of thousands of dollars to restore a single hectare, while the reefs being damaged each year are measured in thousands of square kilometres. He argues that, in many places, the same money would achieve more if spent on reducing farm run-off or enforcing fishing limits. Few restoration practitioners would disagree that their work cannot replace cuts in greenhouse gas emissions. What restoration can do is buy time: maintaining breeding populations, keeping key sites alive for tourism and coastal protection, and preserving genetic diversity that may prove valuable later. Restoration, in other words, is best seen not as a cure but as one part of a much broader effort, and its success will ultimately depend on decisions taken far from any reef.

### Секция 3: Why the Pencil Still Matters

A. Walk into almost any secondary classroom today and you are likely to see more screens than exercise books. Pupils draft essays on laptops, submit homework through online platforms and, increasingly, sit examinations on computers. Against this background, a question that would once have seemed absurd is now asked seriously by parents and policymakers alike: why should children spend hundreds of hours learning to form letters with a pen when they will spend most of their adult lives typing? It is a fair question, and it deserves a better answer than nostalgia. My own view is that handwriting should remain a core part of early education, but for reasons that have little to do with tradition.

B. The case against handwriting is not foolish. Ruth Calloway, who advises schools on digital learning, points out that typing is faster for most fluent users, produces text that is easy to edit and share, and removes a barrier for children whose control of a pencil develops slowly. In her experience, some pupils who struggle to write legibly become far more willing to express themselves once they are allowed a keyboard. She argues that the time spent drilling joined-up letters could be better used teaching pupils to touch-type, search for information and judge the reliability of what they find online. These are genuine skills, and any curriculum that ignores them is failing its students. Calloway is no enemy of the pen; she simply believes its place in the timetable has become too large.

C. Yet her argument rests on an assumption that handwriting is simply a slower way of recording words. There is growing evidence that it is also a way of learning them. Researchers have found that when young children practise forming letters by hand, they tend to recognise those letters more readily afterwards than children who learn them by pressing keys. The likely explanation is that drawing a letter forces the learner to attend to its shape, stroke by stroke, whereas a key produces an identical, finished symbol whatever the hand does. In other words, the effort that makes handwriting slow may be precisely what makes it valuable.

D. A study by the psychologist Annelise Moreau points in a similar direction for older pupils. She asked two groups of fourteen-year-olds to take notes during a short lecture, one group writing by hand and the other using laptops. When tested a week later, the two groups remembered roughly the same number of facts, but the handwriting group performed noticeably better on questions that required them to explain ideas in their own words. Moreau suggests that because people writing by hand cannot keep pace with a speaker, they are obliged to summarise, and summarising requires them to process meaning rather than merely transcribe sound. She is careful to describe her results as preliminary, and her sample was small. Nonetheless, the study fits a pattern that many teachers have long observed informally.

E. One such teacher is Daniyar Petrossian, head of a primary school that reintroduced daily handwriting practice after several years of relying mainly on tablets. He had noticed, he says, that pupils' spelling was growing weaker, partly because automatic correction software fixed their errors before the children were even aware of them. After the change, spelling scores rose steadily over two years, although Petrossian admits that the school also hired an extra reading specialist during the same period, so he cannot be sure which change mattered more. His frankness is welcome. Debates about education are too often settled by anecdote presented as proof, and claims for handwriting deserve the same scrutiny as claims for any new technology.

F. None of this means schools should return to the classrooms of a century ago, where children were punished for untidy loops and every pupil was expected to produce the same elegant script. That approach confused handwriting with penmanship, treating appearance as the goal rather than fluency. What matters is that a child can write quickly and legibly enough that the physical act no longer absorbs their attention, leaving the mind free to concentrate on ideas. Once that point is reached, the decorative extras of traditional cursive add little, and the time saved can reasonably be given to typing.

G. There is also a practical argument that is rarely heard. Technology fails: batteries run flat, networks go down, and examinations in many subjects still require written answers. Teachers who have supervised a room of pupils whose laptops have suddenly lost power will know how quickly a lesson can collapse. A pupil who has never developed a comfortable, reasonably fast hand is at a disadvantage in all of these situations, through no fault of their own.

H. The real choice, then, is not between pen and keyboard but about sequence and balance. Children should learn to write by hand first, because it appears to support the early recognition of letters and words, and they should learn to type well later, because the adult world demands it. Treating the two as rivals has produced a debate that generates more heat than light. Treating them as partners would give children the best of both, and that, rather than a nostalgic defence of the fountain pen, is the case worth making.

## Тест academic-5

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2535. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":7,"high":7}.

### Секция 1: A Noisier World for Wildlife

A. For most of the history of life on Earth, the loudest sounds were natural ones: thunder, waves, the calls of other animals. Today, the hum of roads, the roar of aircraft and the throb of ship engines reach places that were once almost silent. Because noise leaves no visible trace, it was long treated as a nuisance for people rather than as a form of pollution affecting nature. Yet for a great many animals, hearing is not a minor sense. Birds use song to defend territories and attract partners, bats and dolphins find food by listening to echoes, and many prey species depend on detecting the faint rustle of an approaching predator. When human sound drowns out these signals, the consequences can reach far beyond simple annoyance.

B. Some of the clearest evidence comes from cities. Researchers have found that several bird species living near busy roads sing at a higher pitch than members of the same species in quieter areas, lifting their songs above the low rumble of traffic. Others simply sing more loudly, a response also seen in humans, who raise their voices in a crowded restaurant without thinking about it. In some cities, robins have been recorded singing at night, when the streets are calmer. These adjustments show that birds can be remarkably flexible, but flexibility is not the same as being unaffected. A higher or louder song may demand more energy, and it may not carry the same information to a listening female as the song it replaced.

C. Studies like these face a basic problem. A noisy street is also a brightly lit, polluted and heavily built-up one, so it is hard to know whether noise itself is responsible for what scientists observe. To get around this, the field biologist Ottilie Nakamura designed an experiment in a remote woodland with no roads nearby. She placed weatherproof loudspeakers among a group of nest boxes and played recordings of traffic for part of each breeding season, leaving a similar group of boxes undisturbed for comparison. Because the woods were otherwise identical, any difference could be linked to sound alone. In her study, parents nesting near the speakers made fewer feeding visits to their young, apparently spending more time scanning for danger they could no longer hear, and their chicks left the nest at a lower weight. Nakamura is careful to describe the result as a single study of one species, but she argues that the method itself is what matters: it allows noise to be switched on and off like a variable in a laboratory.

D. Beneath the sea, the problem takes a different form. Sound travels much faster and farther in water than in air, which is why many marine animals rely on it more than on sight. Large whales produce low-frequency calls that can be detected across great distances, but the engines and propellers of cargo ships produce noise in much the same frequency range. As a result, the area over which two whales can hear each other may shrink dramatically when a vessel passes, an effect known as masking. There are signs that the noise causes stress as well. On one occasion when shipping in a busy bay was briefly interrupted, researchers found that levels of stress-related hormones in the droppings of local whales fell noticeably compared with previous years.

E. Studying animals that may be hundreds of kilometres away and rarely come to the surface requires patience and technology. The marine acoustician Bayo Lindgren fixed a line of underwater microphones, or hydrophones, to the seabed alongside a major shipping lane and left them recording for eighteen months. No team could listen to so much sound, so he trained computer software to pick out whale calls automatically and then matched them against public records of ship movements. His analysis suggested that whales calling while a large ship was nearby repeated their calls more often, as if trying to make sure they were heard. Lindgren points out, however, that hydrophones only record the animals that call; a whale that falls silent in response to noise is invisible to his equipment, so the true effect may be larger than his data show.

F. Unlike many pollutants, noise has one encouraging feature: it disappears almost as soon as its source is removed. Chemicals can remain in soil and water for decades, but a quiet road is quiet immediately. This means that practical measures can bring rapid benefits. Ships that reduce their speed in sensitive areas produce considerably less underwater noise, and newer propeller designs can make them quieter still. On land, low-noise road surfaces and barriers can reduce disturbance, and construction work can be scheduled to avoid the breeding season. The ecologist Catalina Rourke believes the most important change is a simpler one: recording the natural sounds of a site before any building begins, so that planners know what they stand to lose. As she notes, the quieter places that result are rarely appreciated by wildlife alone.

#### matching_headings: The reading passage has six paragraphs, A–F. Choose the correct heading for each paragraph from the list of headings below. Write the correct number, i–ix. There are more headings than paragraphs.

- **i** A form of pollution that can quickly be undone
- **ii** An approach that isolates noise from other urban factors
- **iii** Species that have benefited from human noise
- **iv** Why sound matters to animals and why it is now under threat
- **v** Long-term underwater recording of whales near ships
- **vi** Birds altering their songs to cope with city noise
- **vii** New laws limiting noise in protected areas
- **viii** How sound behaves at sea and why ships interfere
- **ix** Disagreement over whether city birds sing better

**1.** Paragraph A

- Ключ: **iv (Why sound matters to animals and why it is now under threat)**
- Доказательство: «for a great many animals, hearing is not a minor sense»
- en: Paragraph A explains how animals depend on sound and how human noise now reaches once-silent places. It introduces the whole problem rather than one example.
- ru: Абзац A объясняет, как животные зависят от звука и как шум, создаваемый человеком, теперь доходит до мест, где раньше царила тишина. Он вводит проблему в целом, а не приводит один пример.
- uk: Абзац A пояснює, як тварини залежать від звуку і як шум, створений людиною, тепер сягає місць, де колись панувала тиша. Він представляє проблему загалом, а не наводить один приклад.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: iv ✓; GPT-5.4: iv ✓; Grok: iv ✓.

**2.** Paragraph B

- Ключ: **vi (Birds altering their songs to cope with city noise)**
- Доказательство: «sing at a higher pitch than members of the same species in quieter areas»
- en: Paragraph B describes birds singing higher, louder or at night to be heard over traffic. It does not say any species benefits from noise, and it reports no disagreement among researchers.
- ru: В абзаце B описано, как птицы поют выше, громче или ночью, чтобы их было слышно сквозь шум транспорта. Там не говорится, что какой-либо вид выигрывает от шума, и не упоминаются разногласия между исследователями.
- uk: В абзаці B описано, як птахи співають вище, гучніше або вночі, щоб їх було чути крізь шум транспорту. Там не сказано, що якийсь вид виграє від шуму, і не згадано про розбіжності між дослідниками.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: vi ✓; GPT-5.4: vi ✓; Grok: vi ✓.

**3.** Paragraph C

- Ключ: **ii (An approach that isolates noise from other urban factors)**
- Доказательство: «Because the woods were otherwise identical, any difference could be linked to sound alone.»
- en: Paragraph C is about Nakamura's experiment, which separates the effect of noise from light, pollution and buildings. Its main point is the method that tests noise alone.
- ru: Абзац C посвящён эксперименту Nakamura, который отделяет влияние шума от влияния освещения, загрязнения и застройки. Главное в нём — метод, позволяющий проверить действие одного только шума.
- uk: Абзац C присвячено експерименту Nakamura, який відокремлює вплив шуму від впливу освітлення, забруднення та забудови. Головне в ньому — метод, що дає змогу перевірити дію самого лише шуму.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: ii ✓; GPT-5.4: ii ✓; Grok: ii ✓.

**4.** Paragraph D

- Ключ: **viii (How sound behaves at sea and why ships interfere)**
- Доказательство: «Sound travels much faster and farther in water than in air»
- en: Paragraph D explains how sound travels in water and how ship noise overlaps with whale calls, causing masking and stress. It is about the problem at sea, not a research method.
- ru: Абзац D объясняет, как звук распространяется в воде и как шум судов накладывается на сигналы китов, вызывая маскировку и стресс. Речь идёт о проблеме в море, а не о методе исследования.
- uk: Абзац D пояснює, як звук поширюється у воді і як шум суден накладається на сигнали китів, спричиняючи маскування та стрес. Ідеться про проблему в морі, а не про метод дослідження.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: viii ✓; GPT-5.4: viii ✓; Grok: viii ✓.

**5.** Paragraph E

- Ключ: **v (Long-term underwater recording of whales near ships)**
- Доказательство: «left them recording for eighteen months»
- en: Paragraph E describes Lindgren's hydrophones recording beside a shipping lane and what they showed about whale calls when ships were nearby.
- ru: В абзаце E описаны гидрофоны Lindgren, которые вели запись рядом с судоходным путём, и то, что они показали о сигналах китов, когда поблизости проходили суда.
- uk: В абзаці E описано гідрофони Lindgren, які записували звук поруч із судноплавним шляхом, і те, що вони показали щодо сигналів китів, коли поблизу проходили судна.

**6.** Paragraph F

- Доказательство: «it disappears almost as soon as its source is removed»
- en: Paragraph F stresses that noise, unlike chemicals, stops quickly, so practical measures work fast. Laws are not mentioned, so the heading about new laws is wrong.
- ru: Абзац F подчёркивает, что шум, в отличие от химических веществ, быстро прекращается, поэтому практические меры дают результат сразу. Законы не упоминаются, так что заголовок о новых законах не подходит.
- uk: Абзац F наголошує, що шум, на відміну від хімічних речовин, швидко зникає, тож практичні заходи дають результат одразу. Закони не згадуються, тому заголовок про нові закони не підходить.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: i ✓; GPT-5.4: i ✓; Grok: i ✓.

- Ключ: **C (the shrinking of the range in which whales can communicate.)**
- Доказательство: «the area over which two whales can hear each other may shrink dramatically when a vessel passes, an effect known as masking»
- en: Paragraph D defines masking as the reduced area over which whales can hear each other. Stress is mentioned in the next sentence as a separate effect ('as well').

- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: C ✓; GPT-5.4: C ✓; Grok: C ✓.

### Секция 2: From Scribe to Press: How Printing Changed Reading

A. Before the middle of the fifteenth century, every book in Europe had to be copied by hand. Professional scribes, many of them working in monasteries and later in commercial workshops attached to universities, could spend months producing a single volume. The materials added to the cost: much writing was done on parchment, prepared from animal skins, and a large book might require the skins of well over a hundred animals. As a result, books were valuable possessions, often chained to desks in libraries or listed alongside land and jewellery in wills. Copying also introduced mistakes. Each scribe worked from an earlier copy, so a careless slip could be passed down and multiplied, and two manuscripts of the same work rarely matched word for word. Readers who wanted to compare texts faced a genuine problem: they could not be sure they were reading the same words.

B. The change began in the German city of Mainz around 1450. What emerged there was less a single invention than a clever assembly of existing techniques. Metalworkers already knew how to cast objects in moulds; presses with a large screw were used to squeeze grapes and olives; and paper, which had reached Europe centuries earlier from the Islamic world, had become far cheaper than parchment. The decisive step was to cast individual letters in metal, all of identical height, so that they could be arranged into lines, locked into a frame, inked, printed and then taken apart and reused. A new oil-based ink, which clung to metal better than the water-based inks used by scribes, completed the system. A small team could now produce hundreds of identical pages in the time a scribe needed for a handful.

C. The method spread with remarkable speed. Workers trained in Mainz moved on to other cities, taking their skills with them, and by 1500 printing presses had been set up in more than two hundred towns across Europe. Venice, with its wealthy merchants and its position at the centre of trade routes, became one of the busiest centres of all. Yet printing was a risky business. A printer had to pay for metal, paper and labour long before a single copy was sold, and many early firms went bankrupt. To survive, printers chose titles they were confident would find buyers: prayer books, school grammars, calendars and legal handbooks. Far from being driven only by scholarly ideals, the early book trade followed the market.

D. Printing also altered the appearance and reliability of the book. Because buyers could now choose among competing editions in a shop, printers added features to attract and guide them. Title pages announced the content and often the place where copies could be purchased; page numbers and indexes allowed readers to find a passage quickly and to direct others to it. Smaller formats made books portable and cheaper. At the same time, the process had a double effect on accuracy. A carefully corrected text could be reproduced exactly, so scholars in different cities could at last discuss the same page. But an error that slipped past the printer appeared in every copy of the edition. Over time, the habits of influential printers also encouraged more consistent spelling, although full standardisation came much later.

E. Whether print made more people literate is harder to establish. Literacy in this period left few direct traces, and historians must rely on indirect evidence. One study by the historian Margit Hallström examined lists of possessions drawn up after deaths in several northern European towns during the sixteenth century. She found that books, rare in the homes of craftsmen at the start of the century, appeared in a growing share of them by its end, usually as one or two cheap religious titles. Hallström cautions that owning a book did not prove that anyone in the household could read it. Reading aloud was common, so a single literate family member might share a text with others, and many people who could read had never learned to write.

F. The economic historian Rashid Kalnins argues that the press amplified a demand for reading that already existed rather than creating one from nothing. Towns were growing, merchants needed records, and religious movements urged believers to read scripture in their own languages; printing allowed these pressures to be met on a scale that copying by hand never could. In the 1520s, for example, short pamphlets in German on religious questions were printed in huge numbers and passed from hand to hand. Even so, Kalnins notes, the proportion of Europeans who could read rose slowly, and in most regions it was the gradual expansion of schooling over the following centuries that turned reading into an everyday skill. The press, in his view, was a necessary condition for mass literacy but not a sufficient one.

### Секция 3: The Trouble with Open Offices

A. Walk into almost any modern workplace and you are likely to find the same arrangement: long rows of desks, no internal walls, and managers seated, at least in theory, among their staff. The open-plan office has become so familiar that it is easy to forget it was once a radical idea. Its roots lie partly in a German movement of the late 1950s known as Bürolandschaft, or 'office landscape', which grouped desks organically to reflect how information actually flowed between teams. Today's version, however, owes less to that philosophy than to accountancy. Removing walls allows more employees to fit into less floor space, and the savings on rent can be considerable. Supporters rarely lead with this argument. Instead, they claim that openness encourages conversation, speeds up decisions and breaks down hierarchy. It is these claims, rather than the financial ones, that deserve scrutiny.

B. The central promise is that people who can see one another will talk more. When the organisational researcher Henrik Abalos tracked two firms as they moved from cellular offices into open floors, he found the reverse. Using wearable sensors that recorded when colleagues were within speaking distance, his team observed that face-to-face exchanges fell by roughly two-thirds in the months after the move, while emails and instant messages rose sharply. Abalos's explanation is that people who feel constantly observed seek privacy in whatever way they can: they wear headphones, avoid eye contact and move conversations onto screens. "Remove the walls," he says, "and employees build invisible ones." His study involved only two companies, and he is careful not to generalise too widely, but the pattern is difficult to dismiss.

C. Noise is the most obvious complaint, and also the most misunderstood. Yasmin Thorvald, an acoustics engineer who advises companies on office design, points out that overall volume is rarely the real problem. A steady background hum, such as the sound of ventilation, can actually help concentration. What damages it is intelligible speech: a conversation at the next desk that the brain cannot help following. Thorvald's measurements suggest that a single nearby phone call can be more disruptive than the general noise of a busy café. Carpets and ceiling panels reduce echo, she says, but they cannot stop words from carrying across a few metres of open floor. Her advice to clients is therefore less about absorbing sound than about separating people who need to talk from people who need to think.

D. Distraction has consequences beyond irritation. The environmental psychologist Mercedes Okonjo asked 240 volunteers to complete demanding writing and checking tasks in either a quiet room or a simulated open office. Those in the open setting made more errors on average. More surprisingly, they also reported greater fatigue at the end of the session, including participants whose scores were no worse than those of the quiet group. Okonjo argues that the effort of filtering out unwanted sound is itself tiring, so its cost may be hidden from managers who look only at output. Her volunteers worked on set tasks for a single afternoon, so the effects over months remain unknown. Those who described themselves as easily distracted suffered most, which suggests that a single layout cannot suit everyone equally.

E. None of this means that walls should simply return. Defenders of open plans are right that private offices can isolate people and reinforce status; few would wish to revive the corridor of closed doors with the most senior names on the largest rooms. Nor is collaboration a myth. Some work genuinely benefits from overheard conversation: a junior employee who catches a colleague solving a problem may learn more than any training course could teach. The difficulty is that most office work alternates between such moments and long stretches of solitary concentration, and the standard open floor caters for only one of them.

F. The more promising approach, in my view, is to give employees a choice of settings. Some organisations have experimented with what is often called activity-based working, in which staff move between quiet zones, small enclosed booths for phone calls, and open areas for group work. Okonjo is cautiously supportive of such designs, but she warns that they fail when the quiet spaces are too few or when people feel they must remain visible at their desks to be seen as hard-working. Tariq Mendelsohn, a facilities manager who oversaw one such redesign, found that the booths were booked solid within a week, while a quarter of the open desks sat empty. The lesson he drew was that the company had underestimated the demand for quiet, not that the design had failed.

G. The question, then, is not whether open-plan offices work, but what they are being asked to do. If the aim is to cut costs, they succeed. If the aim is to make people collaborate, the evidence suggests that removing walls is a blunt and sometimes counterproductive tool. Collaboration depends on trust, shared goals and time, none of which can be delivered by a floor plan. Managers who knock down walls in the hope of creating a lively, creative atmosphere are, in effect, asking furniture to do the work of leadership. Organisations that want both conversation and concentration will need to design for both, and accept that the cheapest layout is rarely the most productive one.

Найбільше страждали саме працівники, які легко відволікаються, а не навпаки, і група у відкритому просторі робила більше помилок, а не менше.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: B ✓; GPT-5.4: B ✓; Grok: B ✓.

## Тест academic-6

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2604. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":7,"high":7}.

### Секция 1: Turning the Sea into Tap Water

A. Roughly 97 per cent of the water on Earth lies in the oceans, yet in its natural state it is useless for drinking or irrigation. A litre of typical seawater carries about 35 grams of dissolved salts, far more than the human kidneys can cope with. For most of history, coastal communities facing drought could only gaze at the waves. Today, however, many thousands of desalination plants operate around the world, and in some countries they have turned from a last resort into the backbone of the public supply.

B. Two broad families of technology do the work. The older one relies on heat, imitating the natural cycle in which the sun evaporates seawater and the vapour later falls as rain. In a thermal plant, seawater is boiled, often at reduced pressure so that it boils at a lower temperature, and the steam is condensed on cool surfaces, leaving the salt behind. The method is dependable and tolerates dirty or very salty water, but heating such huge volumes demands enormous amounts of fuel. For that reason, most plants built in recent decades use the second approach, reverse osmosis. Here seawater is forced at high pressure against a membrane so fine that water molecules squeeze through while most dissolved salts are held back.

C. Reverse osmosis sounds simple, but keeping it running is a delicate business. Sand, algae and bacteria in the incoming water would quickly clog the membranes, so the water is first filtered and treated, a stage engineers call pretreatment. The water that emerges on the other side is, in a sense, too pure: lacking minerals, it can corrode pipes, so plants usually add calcium and other minerals back before it reaches consumers. The pressure required, often sixty times that of the atmosphere or more, explains why electricity is the single largest operating cost. Engineers have cut consumption sharply by fitting devices that capture the pressure still held by the salty reject stream and use it to help push the next batch of water through. Even so, producing a cubic metre of desalinated water still takes several times more energy than treating the same amount of river water or groundwater.

D. Where the technology is used reflects geography and wealth. The countries around the Arabian Gulf, with little rainfall, few rivers and abundant oil and gas, were early adopters and remain the largest users; in several of them, most drinking water now comes from the sea. Elsewhere, desalination plays a supporting role. During a prolonged drought in the 2000s, several Australian cities commissioned large plants, only to leave some of them idle for years once the rains returned, a reminder that a plant built as insurance can look like an expensive mistake in wet years. Islands and dry coastal regions from the Mediterranean to California have also invested, while poorer inland areas, far from the sea and short of money, have largely been left out.

E. The environmental costs begin at the intake. Pipes drawing in vast quantities of seawater can trap fish against screens and suck in eggs and larvae, which rarely survive. Some newer plants instead take water from wells dug beneath the seabed, using the sand as a natural filter, although suitable ground is not found on every coast. A greater concern lies at the other end of the process. For every litre of fresh water produced, a plant typically discharges a similar or larger volume of brine, roughly twice as salty as the sea and sometimes carrying traces of the chemicals used in cleaning. Being denser than the surrounding water, brine tends to sink and spread across the seabed, where it can harm creatures that cannot move away.

F. How serious that harm is remains disputed. Soraya Abdelnour, a marine ecologist, spent three years surveying the seabed around two outfalls on the same coastline. Where brine was released through diffusers, nozzles that spray it upwards so that it mixes rapidly with seawater, changes in seabed life were confined to a narrow zone a few hundred metres wide. Where it simply poured from an open pipe, the affected area was considerably larger. Abdelnour cautions, however, that her study covered a coast with strong currents, and that in sheltered bays brine would probably be diluted far less efficiently.

G. Then there is the climate. Where the electricity comes from burning fossil fuels, every glass of desalinated water adds to greenhouse emissions, worsening the very shifts in rainfall that make desalination necessary. Pairing plants with solar or wind power offers one way out, and because fresh water, unlike electricity, can be stored cheaply in reservoirs, plants can run harder when power is plentiful. Mikkel Oyarzún, an engineer who designs such systems, argues that the remaining obstacle is financial rather than technical: plants are costly to build, and their output is hard to justify when rain is abundant. Desalination, in his view, works best alongside repairing leaking pipes and recycling wastewater, not as a substitute for using water wisely.

#### true_false_not_given: Do the following statements agree with the information given in the Reading Passage? Write TRUE if the statement agrees with the information, FALSE if the statement contradicts the information, or NOT GIVEN if there is no information on this.

**1.** The salt content of seawater is too high for the human body to process safely.

- Ключ: **TRUE**
- Доказательство: «far more than the human kidneys can cope with»
- en: Paragraph A says seawater has far more salt than human kidneys can cope with. This is the same idea in different words.

- uk: В абзаці A сказано, що в морській воді значно більше солі, ніж здатні витримати нирки людини. Це та сама думка, лише іншими словами.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: T ✓; GPT-5.4: T ✓; Grok: T ✓.

**2.** Thermal desalination only works well if the seawater is fairly clean.

- Ключ: **FALSE**
- Доказательство: «tolerates dirty or very salty water»
- en: Paragraph B says the thermal method tolerates dirty or very salty water, so it does not need clean water. The statement says the opposite.

- uk: В абзаці B сказано, що термічний метод дає раду брудній або дуже солоній воді, тобто чиста вода йому не потрібна. У твердженні сказано протилежне.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: F ✓; GPT-5.4: F ✓; Grok: F ✓.

**3.** Water leaving reverse osmosis membranes typically contains too many minerals to be used straight away.

- Ключ: **FALSE**
- Доказательство: «too pure: lacking minerals, it can corrode pipes»
- en: Paragraph C says the water is too pure and lacks minerals, so minerals are added back. The problem is too few minerals, not too many.

- uk: В абзаці C сказано, що вода виходить надто чистою і в ній бракує мінералів, тому їх додають назад. Проблема в тому, що мінералів замало, а не забагато.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: F ✓; GPT-5.4: F ✓; Grok: F ✓.

**4.** Reverse osmosis membranes usually need replacing every few years.

- Доказательство: — (NOT GIVEN)

- uk: В абзаці C сказано, що мембрани можуть забиватися піском, водоростями й бактеріями, тому воду попередньо очищають. Але там нічого не сказано про те, скільки служать мембрани чи коли їх треба міняти.

**5.** In certain Gulf states, the majority of drinking water is now obtained from seawater.

- en: Paragraph D says that in several Gulf countries most drinking water now comes from the sea. 'The majority' means 'most'.

### Секция 2: Why the Wait Feels Long

A. Few experiences are as universal, or as quietly frustrating, as standing in a queue. The line might lead to a supermarket checkout, a hospital reception desk or a theme-park ride. Either way, most people have felt a ten-minute wait drag on as though it were half an hour, and an equally long one pass almost unnoticed. Psychologists who study queues explain this by separating two kinds of time: the duration a clock would record and the duration a person actually experiences. For the organisations that make customers wait, the second figure frequently matters more than the first. Satisfaction depends less on how long people stand in line than on how long they believe they have stood there.

B. One of the most reliable findings is that empty time feels longer than occupied time. When there is nothing to do, attention turns to the passing of time itself, and every minute becomes noticeable. This is why so many waiting areas offer something to look at or handle. Hotels and office buildings often hang mirrors beside lifts. People waiting for the doors to open can check their appearance or discreetly observe others, and complaints about slow lifts tend to fall as a result. A related effect concerns the moment at which a service seems to begin. People who are still waiting to be acknowledged feel more impatient than those who sense that the process is under way. This is why a restaurant that hands diners a menu as soon as they sit down makes them feel that their meal has already started, even if the kitchen has not yet received the order.

C. Uncertainty is another powerful influence. A wait of unknown length feels longer than one whose end is in sight, and a delay that nobody explains feels longer than one with an obvious cause. Both conditions produce anxiety, and anxiety appears to stretch time: the worried mind keeps checking whether anything has changed. In one study, the psychologist Orla Fenwick asked passengers on a train held outside a station to estimate how long they had been stationary. In some carriages the driver had made a brief announcement explaining the hold-up, and passengers there guessed, on average, several minutes less than those in carriages where the speakers had failed. Yet both groups had waited exactly the same time. Fenwick cautions that her sample came from a single railway line, but the result fits a wider pattern: information, even unwelcome information, makes waiting easier to bear.

D. Perhaps the strongest feelings in any queue concern fairness. Most cultures expect queues to follow the principle of first come, first served, and seeing someone who arrived later reach the front first can cause more irritation than the wait itself. Multiple parallel lines, common in older supermarkets, make such injustices highly visible, since one line inevitably moves faster than its neighbours. A single line that feeds several counters removes the problem, because every customer is served strictly in order of arrival. People tend to rate it as fairer even when the average wait is no shorter. The arrangement has a weakness, however. A single line serving many counters can grow very long, and newcomers who see it may decide not to join at all. For this reason some firms break it up with ropes and bends, so that no one can see its full length at once.

E. These principles can be combined, as a project led by Bilal Haddad, the operations manager of a busy passport office, illustrates. Haddad began by asking his team to spend a month observing the public hall. They found that complaints rose sharply whenever visitors could see no sign of progress. Their first change was to replace the five separate lines in front of the counters with one single line that served all of them. Next, they installed screens displaying the expected wait. The estimates were kept deliberately pessimistic, so that most visitors would be called sooner than predicted and feel pleasantly surprised. Visitors were then given a numbered ticket, which allowed them to wait in a nearby café rather than stand in the hall. Finally, each person was asked to fill in a short form while waiting, which kept them occupied and also reduced the time needed at the counter. After six months, the average wait had barely changed, yet complaints had fallen by half.

F. Not everyone welcomes this kind of management. Yuna Sorensen, a consumer researcher, argues that techniques designed to shorten perceived time can become a substitute for shortening real time. An organisation that installs entertaining screens, she points out, may feel less pressure to employ the extra staff who would actually reduce delays. She is also uneasy about deliberately pessimistic estimates, which may persuade some customers to give up and leave before they have joined the queue at all. Her criticisms do not mean that the psychology of waiting should be ignored. Rather, they suggest that the most successful approach combines genuine improvements in speed with honest information and thoughtful design, so that customers wait less and also feel that they have waited less.

### Секция 3: In Praise of Failure

A. Open almost any scientific journal and you will find a remarkable run of success. Hypotheses are confirmed, new effects are detected and promising treatments appear to work. Anyone who has spent time in a laboratory knows that this is not what research actually looks like. Many experiments do not go to plan: reactions refuse to start, cells die for no obvious reason and carefully predicted effects simply fail to appear. The journals, in other words, offer something closer to a highlight reel than a full recording of the match. My argument is that the missing footage matters, and that science would be healthier, faster and cheaper if failed experiments and negative results were treated as part of the record rather than as private embarrassments.

B. The imbalance is not imagined. Researchers who study the scientific literature itself have repeatedly found that published papers report positive results far more often than negative ones. In one recent analysis, the meta-researcher Sunil Varghese and his team examined around 1,500 studies in nutrition and ecology and found that more than nine in ten claimed to support the hypothesis the authors had set out to test. It would be pleasant to conclude that scientists are unusually gifted at predicting nature. A far more plausible explanation is that studies which find nothing are quietly left unpublished, a problem long known as the 'file drawer' effect.

C. The cost of those drawers is easy to underestimate. Consider Kaito Morimoto, a doctoral student in materials science, who spent eight months trying to grow a particular crystal using a technique described in a promising conference talk. Only when he mentioned his difficulties at a workshop did he discover that at least three other groups had tried the same method and given up. None had reported the attempt, so each group had paid for the same lesson separately. Multiply that experience across thousands of laboratories and the waste becomes considerable. There is a subtler danger too. When researchers pool the results of many studies to judge whether a medical treatment works, they usually include only the studies that were published. If the disappointing ones are missing, the combined estimate makes the treatment look more effective than it really is, and doctors may make decisions on the basis of an exaggerated benefit.

D. History offers reminders of how valuable a null result can be. In 1887, two American physicists built an apparatus to detect the 'ether', the invisible medium through which light was then thought to travel. Far from being a careless piece of work, their instrument was among the most sensitive of its day, yet it detected no trace of the ether. The apparent failure later came to be seen as important evidence for the theory of special relativity. The historian of science Ximena Arroyo argues that journals of that era printed accounts of unsuccessful experiments more readily than today's journals do. In her view, the change came during the twentieth century, as the competition for research funding and for citations grew fiercer and novelty became the quality that editors prized above all.

E. Why, then, do scientists keep their failures to themselves? Part of the answer is incentives: a negative result rarely leads to a prestigious publication, and careers are built on publications. But there is also a more respectable reason, and it deserves to be taken seriously. An experiment can fail because the idea behind it is wrong, or simply because the equipment was faulty or the technique was clumsy. Publishing every disappointment could fill the literature with noise. I accept this concern, but I do not think it justifies silence. The answer is to describe methods in enough detail that others can judge whether a failure is informative. The chemist Odette Lamarque has shown what this might look like. Her group keeps a shared archive of every reaction that did not work, with notes on conditions and suspected causes, and new members must read it before they begin their own projects. She reports that several entries, revisited years later with better equipment, became the starting points for successful studies.

F. Some institutions are beginning to move in the same direction. A number of journals now offer a format in which a study is accepted for publication on the strength of its question and design, before any data have been collected; whatever the results, the paper appears. Agata Wiśniewska, who edits one such journal, says that in her experience the format has changed how reviewers behave. Unable to see the outcome, they examine the methods far more critically, because the quality of the design is the only thing left to judge. Online repositories for negative results have also appeared, and some funding bodies now expect researchers to report the outcomes of projects they paid for, including the unwelcome ones.

G. None of this means that failure should be celebrated for its own sake. A sloppy experiment that goes wrong teaches nobody anything, and praising failure must not become an excuse for careless work. Nor does every unsuccessful study need to become a full journal article; often a short, searchable record of what was tried and what happened would be enough. What matters is that the knowledge exists somewhere other than in one researcher's memory. Science advances by ruling things out as much as by finding things in, and a discipline that hides half of its results is working with only half of its evidence.

## Тест academic-7

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2591. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":7,"high":7}.

### Секция 1: Mangroves on the Front Line

A. Along sheltered tropical and subtropical coastlines, where rivers meet the sea, a peculiar kind of forest stands with its feet in salt water. Mangroves are not a single species but a collection of trees and shrubs, drawn from several unrelated plant families, that have independently evolved ways of surviving conditions that would kill most land plants. With each high tide, seawater floods their roots; as the tide retreats, it exposes soft, airless mud. Some species cope by filtering salt out at the roots, while others take it in and excrete it through glands on their leaves. Many send up pencil-like roots from the mud, which act as snorkels, allowing the buried root system to breathe. Others are propped up on arching stilt roots that make the forest look as though it is walking into the water.

B. It is this tangle of roots and trunks that makes mangroves so valuable to the people who live behind them. When waves enter a mangrove belt, they lose energy through friction as water is forced around countless obstacles. In a study on a low-lying delta coast, coastal engineer Nirmala Seneviratne placed pressure sensors at intervals through a forest 300 metres wide and recorded conditions over two monsoon seasons. She found that the height of ordinary waves fell by around 60 per cent between the seaward edge and the landward edge. Seneviratne is careful not to overstate the result. Exceptionally powerful storm surges, she notes, can push water through even a healthy forest, and a narrow fringe of trees offers far less protection than a broad one. Nevertheless, in her view, a wide belt can do much of the work of a concrete sea wall at a fraction of the cost, and unlike a wall it repairs itself.

C. Mangroves also shape the land itself. Their roots slow the movement of water so that fine particles carried by rivers and tides settle out instead of being washed away. Over time this trapped sediment, together with decaying roots, raises the forest floor. Where enough sediment is available, the ground can build up quickly enough to keep pace with the rising sea, something no artificial barrier can do. The same waterlogged soils lock away remarkable quantities of carbon, because dead plant material decomposes very slowly in the absence of oxygen. Researchers have found that, area for area, mangrove forests can store several times as much carbon as many forests on dry land, mostly in their soils. The shallow channels between the roots, meanwhile, shelter young fish, shrimp and crabs, many of which are later caught by fishers once they move into open water.

D. Despite these benefits, mangroves have been cleared on a vast scale. A substantial share of the world's mangrove area has disappeared since the middle of the twentieth century. In parts of Southeast Asia and Latin America, a major cause was the spread of shrimp farming: forests were cut down and replaced by rectangular ponds enclosed by earth walls. Such ponds were often productive for only a few years before disease and accumulated waste forced owners to abandon them and clear new ground. Elsewhere, mangroves gave way to rice paddies, oil palm plantations, ports and tourist resorts, or were thinned for timber and charcoal. Because the forests occupied land that seemed to have no owner and no obvious use, their destruction attracted little attention until coastal communities began to notice increased flooding and smaller catches. The rate of loss has slowed considerably in recent years, partly because the value of the forests is now more widely understood.

E. Replanting has become popular with governments, charities and companies, but early efforts produced disappointing results. Ecologist Esteban Ruvalcaba visited 46 restoration sites along one stretch of coast and counted how many trees were still alive five years after planting. Where volunteers had pushed seedlings into open mudflats below the natural forest edge, survival was often under 10 per cent: the young trees spent too long underwater each day and were swept away or drowned. Projects that planted a single fast-growing species in neat rows did somewhat better but created plantations that supported few animals. The most successful sites, Ruvalcaba found, were abandoned shrimp ponds where the earth walls had simply been broken open. Once tidal water could flow in and out again, seeds arrived naturally from nearby forests, and after five years these sites held a richer mix of species than any of the planted ones. His conclusion is that restoring the right water conditions often matters more than planting itself.

F. Even a well-designed project can fail if the people living nearby see no reason to protect it. Social scientist Adaeze Nwosu compared villages where restoration had been organised by outside agencies with villages where residents had helped to choose the sites and set the rules. In the second group, young forests were far less likely to be cut down again within a decade. Nwosu points in particular to agreements that gave local families the right to collect crabs from the restored areas, which turned the forest into a source of income worth defending. Her work suggests that mangroves will only hold the line against the sea if they are also valued by those who live on the shore.
