### Секция 2: From Chains to Satellites

A. Until well into the twentieth century, a detailed map of a country began with people walking across it. Surveyors carried measuring chains, heavy brass instruments and notebooks, and they depended on triangulation, a technique borrowed from geometry. A team would first measure a single straight line on flat ground, called a baseline, with great care, sometimes over several weeks. From each end of this line they sighted a distant landmark, such as a church tower or a hilltop, and recorded the angles. Because one side and two angles are enough to fix the rest of a triangle, the surveyors could calculate distances they had never walked, then use each new side as the starting point for another triangle. In this way a chain of triangles gradually spread across the landscape.

B. The method was accurate but painfully slow. Large national surveys in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries took decades to complete, and survey parties endured heat, disease and harsh weather while hauling equipment up hills. Yet according to Anneke Vos, a historian of cartography, the physical hardship is only part of the story. After studying the field notebooks of several surveys, she argues that the finished maps concealed countless small decisions: which villages to record, whose spelling of a place name to adopt, and which paths were important enough to draw. Some of these choices, she adds, were made by surveyors who did not speak the local language. 'A map looks like a neutral picture,' she writes, 'but every line on it was chosen by someone standing in a particular place.'

C. The first great acceleration came from the air. After the First World War, cameras mounted in aircraft were used to photograph large areas in overlapping strips. Specialists then examined pairs of these images through stereoscopes, instruments that made the ground appear three-dimensional and allowed the height of hills and valleys to be measured. This technique, known as photogrammetry, did not abolish fieldwork – someone still had to visit the ground to name features and check fixed points – but it reduced the walking dramatically. Aerial photographs also captured details, such as the precise outline of a coastline or the edge of a forest, that a surveyor on foot could easily misjudge. A district that might once have occupied a survey party for several seasons could now be photographed in a few days.

D. Satellites changed the scale once again. From 1972, satellites designed to study the Earth's surface began sending back regular images of almost every part of the planet, allowing map-makers to track changes such as spreading cities, retreating glaciers and new roads. Later, satellite navigation systems let anyone with a receiver work out their position by timing signals from several satellites at once. For years civilian users received a deliberately degraded signal, but in 2000 this restriction was switched off, and ordinary receivers became accurate to within a few metres. Efosa Delgado, a geographer who compared old triangulation points with their modern satellite positions, found that many nineteenth-century measurements were out by less than a metre. He attributes this not to the quality of the surveyors' instruments but to their habit of repeating every angle many times and averaging the results.

E. Perhaps the deepest change, however, lies not in how information is collected but in how it is stored. A printed map is a finished object; once the ink is dry, correcting it means printing a new edition. Digital mapping systems instead keep information in separate layers – roads, rivers, buildings, land use – that can be combined, updated or hidden at will. This has made maps far cheaper to revise, but it has also shifted the main problem from gathering information to managing it. Shirin Farouk, who studies volunteer mapping projects, notes that thousands of unpaid contributors now add streets and buildings to online maps. In her research on one coastal region, volunteers recorded footpaths and small shops that appeared on no government map. She also found, however, that coverage was uneven: wealthy urban districts were mapped in fine detail, while poorer rural areas received far less attention.

F. The way people use maps has changed as well. Pavel Kimura, who researches human navigation, asked two groups of walkers to cross an unfamiliar town, one following turn-by-turn directions on a phone and the other using a paper map. Those guided by the phone arrived slightly sooner and made fewer wrong turns, but when asked afterwards to sketch their route, they produced far less accurate drawings. Kimura suggests that when a device takes over the work of orientation, people pay less attention to landmarks, although he cautions that his study involved only short journeys.

G. For all these changes, some things remain remarkably constant. Satellite positions are still checked against fixed markers on the ground, and map-makers continue to face the questions that troubled their predecessors: what to include, what to leave out and what to call it. The tools have moved from the hillside into orbit, but the judgement behind every map is still a human one.

### Секция 3: Fewer Days, Better Work?

A. For most of the last century, the five-day week has seemed as fixed as the calendar itself. Yet it is a social invention, not a law of nature: the two-day weekend became common only after long campaigns by workers and a recognition by some employers that rested staff were more productive. Today a growing number of organisations are asking whether the next step is a third day off. My own view is that the idea deserves to be taken seriously, but not in the form in which it is often sold. A four-day week is worth having only if it changes how work is done, not merely when it is done.

B. The case in favour begins with a simple observation: hours at a desk are not the same as useful output. Sunniva Halvorsen, an organisational psychologist, tracked forty office teams over a year and found that a large share of the working day was consumed by meetings that participants themselves rated as unnecessary. When her teams moved to four days while keeping full pay, most cut their meetings by around a third and reported that they protected long blocks of uninterrupted time for demanding tasks. Output, measured by projects completed on schedule, remained broadly stable. Halvorsen is careful to note that her teams volunteered for the trial, so they may have been unusually motivated, but the pattern is hard to dismiss.

C. Health is the second argument. Mei-Ling Arkwright, who studies occupational wellbeing, compared sickness absence in firms before and after a reduction in days. Absence fell in almost every case, and staff described sleeping better and feeling less exhausted on Monday mornings. Arkwright points out, however, that the benefits were concentrated among employees who genuinely stopped working on their extra day. Those who continued to answer emails from home reported little improvement. In other words, a day off that is not really off brings few gains. Her conclusion is that a reduced week works best when managers make it clear that nobody is expected to be reachable on the free day.

D. It is here that the most common version of the policy goes wrong. Many employers, attracted by the headline but nervous about lost hours, introduce a "compressed" week, in which the same forty hours are squeezed into four ten-hour days. Adebayo Whitcombe, a labour economist, argues that this simply moves fatigue around. In his study of warehouse and call-centre staff on compressed schedules, error rates rose sharply in the final two hours of each long shift, wiping out much of the benefit of the extra rest day. I find his evidence persuasive. A shorter week that is achieved by making each day longer misunderstands why fewer hours can help in the first place.

E. Critics raise a different objection: that the four-day week suits only certain kinds of job. A software developer can perhaps finish the same work in less time; a nurse cannot care for patients who are not there. Jonas Esterhuizen, a consultant who works with service organisations, has observed that hospitals, schools and shops must keep their doors open regardless of staff schedules, so a reduced week for each worker means hiring more people or arranging complicated rotas. He describes the cost of this extra staffing as the "hidden bill" that enthusiastic reports tend to ignore. This is a fair point, and supporters who claim the policy will pay for itself everywhere are, in my view, overstating their case.

F. A further concern is fairness within the workforce. Paulina Ndiaye, a sociologist, interviewed employees at firms that had adopted four-day schedules for some departments but not others. She found that resentment grew quickly where front-line staff kept five days while head-office colleagues enjoyed three-day weekends. Several interviewees said the arrangement made them feel like second-class employees, even when their pay was unchanged. Ndiaye suggests that employers should offer comparable benefits, such as shorter shifts or additional annual leave, to groups whose roles cannot be reorganised. Whether such alternatives are valued as highly as a full extra day off remains, she admits, an open question.

G. None of these difficulties seems to me a reason to abandon the idea. They are reasons to introduce it carefully. The organisations that have succeeded tend to share three features: they began with a limited trial, they measured results rather than hours, and they removed tasks instead of compressing them. Those that failed usually tried to change everything at once, or quietly raised targets so that staff ended up working on their supposed day off. Governments could help by funding trials in sectors where the practical obstacles are greatest, since these are precisely the settings about which we know least.

H. The five-day week was once a radical proposal too. Its defenders did not argue that every job could be organised identically; they argued that people work better when they have time to recover. That principle still holds, perhaps more than ever in an age of constant digital connection. The question is not whether we can afford a four-day week, but whether we are willing to do the harder work of deciding which of our current tasks actually matter.

## Тест academic-8

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2540. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":7,"high":7}.

### Секция 1: Whispers in the Soil and Air

A. Few scientific ideas have travelled as quickly from the laboratory to the bookshop as the claim that plants talk to one another. Documentaries describe forests as communities whose members share food and trade warnings, and the language of conversation has become almost routine in popular writing about nature. Many botanists, however, are uneasy. They do not deny that plants exchange chemical substances, often in ways that affect their neighbours. Their objection is to the word 'talk' itself, which implies a speaker who intends to be understood and a listener who interprets a message. Applied to organisms without brains, such vocabulary can make modest findings sound revolutionary, and it can hide the real question, which is not whether information passes between plants but who, if anyone, gains from its passage.

B. The strongest evidence concerns signals carried through the air. When insects chew a leaf, many plants release a blend of volatile organic compounds, light molecules that evaporate easily and drift on the breeze. Researchers have found that undamaged plants exposed to this blend often respond by preparing their own defences: they may not produce protective chemicals straight away, but when insects later attack, they manufacture toxins or bitter compounds faster and in greater quantities than plants that received no warning. This state of readiness is known as priming. The first reports of the effect, published in the early 1980s, were greeted with considerable suspicion, partly because the experiments were small. Since then, however, similar responses have been recorded in dozens of species, from wild tobacco to maize, and few specialists now doubt that the phenomenon is real.

C. Whether this amounts to communication is another matter. A warning is only a message if sending it brings some advantage to the sender, and it is hard to see why a damaged plant would help potential competitors growing beside it. One explanation, which many ecologists find persuasive, is that the airborne compounds evolved for internal use. Chemicals travelling through a plant's veins move slowly, so a branch under attack may alert other branches of the same plant more quickly through the air. Neighbours, on this view, are not being addressed at all; they are simply eavesdropping. A field study by the ecologist Corinne Abeyta lends some support to the idea. Working with wild mustard, she found that neighbouring plants responded to the compounds only when they grew within about sixty centimetres of the damaged one, a distance suited to signalling within a single bushy plant rather than across a meadow.

D. Below ground, the story involves a third party. The great majority of land plants form partnerships with mycorrhizal fungi, whose fine threads, called hyphae, wrap around or penetrate roots. The arrangement is essentially a trade: the fungus gathers water and minerals such as phosphorus from the soil, which it passes to the plant in exchange for sugars produced by photosynthesis. Because a single fungus can colonise the roots of several plants at once, neighbouring trees may be joined by what scientists call a common mycorrhizal network. In laboratory and greenhouse experiments, carbon labelled so that it could be traced has been seen moving from one seedling to another through such connections. These results inspired the phrase 'wood wide web' and, in popular accounts, a striking picture of large 'mother trees' that feed shaded young trees and send them chemical warnings of danger.

E. It is this picture that has attracted the sharpest criticism. When the soil scientist Matiu Rangihau and his colleagues re-examined the field studies most often cited in its support, they concluded that the evidence was far weaker than its reputation suggested. In many experiments, substances could have travelled between trees by routes that had nothing to do with fungi: dissolved in soil water, for instance, or through roots that had grown into direct contact. Few studies had been designed to rule these possibilities out. Even where carbon clearly did pass through fungal threads, the quantities were usually tiny, and it was often unclear whether the carbon reached the leaves and stems of the receiving tree or simply remained inside the fungus. Rangihau also pointed out that fungi have interests of their own. If resources move through a network, he argued, the fungus may be directing them for its own benefit rather than acting as a loyal messenger between trees.

F. Resolving the dispute will require experiments that separate the possible routes. The plant physiologist Halvard Ishikawa has proposed surrounding the roots of young trees with mesh with holes of different sizes: one coarse enough to let fungal threads through while keeping roots apart, and one so fine that it blocks the threads as well. If transfer continues behind the finer mesh, fungi cannot be responsible. Ishikawa also warns against letting the debate shape forestry policy too early. Protecting large old trees may well be sensible, he notes, but for reasons such as habitat and carbon storage rather than because of unproven claims about underground networks. Meanwhile, the broader conclusion stands: plants clearly detect and react to their neighbours. Whether that deserves to be called talking remains, for now, an open question.

### Секция 2: Open to All: The Long Story of the Public Library

A. For most of recorded history, a large collection of books was a sign of power rather than a public service. The royal library at Nineveh, which held thousands of clay tablets, and the famous library of Alexandria were built to serve rulers, priests and scholars, and ordinary people had no reason to expect entry. In medieval Europe, the most important collections belonged to monasteries and universities, where valuable manuscripts were sometimes chained to desks to prevent theft. Even after the printing press made books far cheaper from the fifteenth century onwards, the great libraries of princes and aristocrats remained private spaces, opened only as a favour to visitors judged respectable. A library, in other words, was something to be guarded, not shared.

B. The first steps towards wider access came not from governments but from readers themselves. In eighteenth-century Britain and its American colonies, groups of merchants, clerks and craftsmen formed subscription libraries, pooling their money to buy books that none of them could have afforded alone. Members paid an entrance fee and an annual charge, and in return could borrow from a shared stock. At the same time, booksellers ran commercial circulating libraries, renting out popular titles, especially novels, by the week. Later, mechanics' institutes offered lectures and reading rooms to skilled workers. These arrangements greatly widened the reading public, yet they all depended on the ability to pay, and the poorest households, who had the least access to books at home, were largely left out.

C. The idea that a library should be free to every resident, and paid for by the community as a whole, took hold in the middle of the nineteenth century. Reformers argued that access to good books would encourage self-improvement, produce more capable workers and keep working men out of the public house. In 1850 a British law permitted town councils to fund a library through a local tax, although the sum allowed was tiny and many towns delayed for years before adopting the measure. Across the Atlantic, Boston opened a large free municipal library in 1854, and other American cities soon followed. The reformers' hopes were not always met. When the historian Philippa Okoye examined the borrowing registers of one northern English town library from the 1870s, she found that around three-quarters of loans were novels rather than the works of history and science the founders had in mind. Okoye argues, however, that this popularity was the library's salvation: heavy use made it far easier for councils to justify the tax.

D. What turned scattered experiments into a national and then international network was private wealth. Between the 1880s and the 1920s, a Scottish-born steel industrialist paid for more than 2,500 library buildings, mainly in the English-speaking world. His gifts came with a condition: the town had to provide the land and commit to annual funding for books and staff, so that the building would not become an empty monument once the donation had been spent. The new buildings also changed how libraries were used. Earlier libraries had generally kept books behind counters, so that readers had to request a title from an assistant; many of the new ones introduced open shelves, allowing visitors to browse freely, and set aside separate rooms for children.

E. During the twentieth century the library's role widened well beyond lending. Branches ran classes in reading and languages for new immigrants, hosted public lectures and offered quiet space to students who had none at home. Mobile libraries, vans fitted with shelves, carried books to villages and farms too remote to support a branch of their own. In wartime and during economic depressions, libraries recorded rising numbers of visitors, as people out of work came in search of information, warmth or simply somewhere to spend the day without paying.

F. The arrival of the internet led many commentators to predict that libraries would soon become obsolete: if information could be found online, why visit a building full of paper? The prediction has not come true, though the library has had to reinvent itself once again. Today a typical branch lends e-books as well as printed ones, offers free computers and internet connections, helps people complete official forms that are now available only online, and may even provide 3D printers and recording equipment. A survey of visitors in four cities by the sociologist Jun Takeda found that fewer than half of them had borrowed anything on the day they were questioned. The survey led him to argue that the library's most valuable asset is no longer its collection but its status as one of the last indoor spaces anyone can enter without being expected to spend money. Seen this way, the modern library is less a departure from its nineteenth-century origins than a return to them: the promise of access for all.

### Секция 3: The Surprising Greenness of the City

A. Ask most people to picture an environmentally friendly life and they will describe something rural: a cottage with a vegetable patch, a wood-burning stove, a view of fields rather than traffic. The city, by contrast, is imagined as a machine for consuming resources, its skyline smudged with exhaust fumes. It is an appealing picture, and I believe it is largely the wrong way round. Measured per person rather than per square kilometre, a well-planned city is one of the least damaging places a human being can live. The countryside has many virtues, but a small carbon footprint is rarely among them.

B. The clearest evidence comes from transport. Outside towns, shops, schools and workplaces are scattered, and buses, where they exist at all, may run only a few times a day. The result is that almost every errand requires a car, and often a long drive. For many rural families, a second car is not a luxury but a necessity. In a dense city the same errands can be done on foot, by bicycle or by train. When the transport geographer Seren Okubo compared week-long travel diaries kept by households in a large city and in the villages surrounding it, she found that village households drove roughly three times as far. Crucially, the gap remained after she had adjusted for differences in income and household size, which suggests that it was the layout of the places, not the character of the people, that made the difference.

C. Housing tells a similar story. A detached house in the country is exposed to the weather on every side, so heat escapes through four outer walls and a roof. A flat in an apartment block may have only one or two walls facing the outside air; the rest are shared with neighbours whose own heating helps keep it warm. City homes also tend to be smaller, and smaller spaces need less energy to heat, light and fill with furniture. Less visible, but just as important, is the network that supports every home. A kilometre of water pipe, electricity cable or paved road in a crowded district serves hundreds of households; in a rural area the same kilometre might serve a handful. That means less material, less maintenance and less energy spent pumping water over long distances.

D. The strongest objection to this argument concerns consumption. The economist Raúl Fenstermacher has pointed out that most comparisons count only the emissions produced within a place, such as the fuel burned in its cars and boilers. They ignore the emissions created elsewhere to make the clothes, electronics and holidays that residents buy. When he estimated footprints from household spending instead, the wealthiest city districts in his sample came out worse than the rural average, mainly because of frequent air travel. This is a serious point, and it should humble anyone who assumes that city dwellers are automatically virtuous. Yet it does not show that cities are the problem. What Fenstermacher measured is the effect of money: high earners fly more wherever they live, and a prosperous family in a converted farmhouse is no exception. Compare households with similar incomes, and the city's advantage in heating and travel reappears.

E. There is also the question of land. Cities cover only a small fraction of the earth's land surface, yet they house more than half of its people. If those people were spread thinly across the countryside, each with a garden and a driveway, far more forest, wetland and grassland would be lost to houses and roads. Ironically, then, the people who love nature most might serve it best by living at a distance from it. The real danger lies in between. Low-density suburbs, with their wide roads and long distances to the nearest shop, combine the car dependence of the countryside with the loss of open land that comes with building — in many ways the worst of both worlds.

F. None of this means that cities are pleasant or healthy by default. Dense streets trap heat, so that on summer nights a city centre can remain several degrees warmer than nearby farmland, and traffic still fills the air with harmful pollutants. The urban planner Thandiwe Corrigan argues that such problems are routinely blamed on density itself when they are really failures of design. Her work in several mid-sized cities has focused on street trees, green roofs and the removal of through traffic from residential areas, measures that she says can make crowded neighbourhoods markedly cooler and quieter. I find her argument persuasive: a badly designed city is an argument for better cities, not for leaving them.

G. The countryside is not the villain of this story. Food has to be grown somewhere, and farmers, foresters and those who look after rural land cannot do their work from an apartment. But those who move to a village believing they are lightening their impact on the planet are, in most cases, mistaken. The more useful task for governments is to make dense living attractive — with good schools, safe streets, affordable flats and parks within walking distance — so that choosing the greener option no longer feels like a sacrifice.

## Тест academic-9

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2558. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":7,"high":7}.

### Секция 1: Listening to Volcanoes

A. To the people who live beneath them, volcanoes can seem silent for generations. Yet a volcano that appears asleep is rarely completely quiet. Deep beneath the surface, molten rock, or magma, pushes against the surrounding crust, gases escape through cracks, and heated groundwater moves through fractured rock. Each of these processes produces signals that can be detected, provided the right instruments are in place. Over the past century, volcanologists have learned to interpret many of these signals, and their work has saved thousands of lives. Even so, predicting exactly when, or even whether, a restless volcano will erupt remains one of the most stubborn problems in the earth sciences.

B. The most widely used tool is the seismometer, an instrument that records vibrations in the ground. As magma forces its way upwards, it breaks rock and produces swarms of small earthquakes, most too weak to be felt by humans. Scientists pay particular attention to their depth: a cluster of earthquakes that gradually becomes shallower may indicate that magma is approaching the surface. Some volcanoes also generate a continuous, low humming known as tremor, which is thought to arise when fluids flow through underground channels. Because these signals are so varied, an experienced analyst often needs to examine several days of recordings before deciding whether a change is significant.

C. A second approach is to measure the shape of the volcano itself. When magma accumulates in a chamber beneath a mountain, the ground above can swell by centimetres or, occasionally, by metres. Before the 1980 eruption of Mount St Helens in the United States, part of the northern side of the mountain bulged outwards dramatically. Today, deformation is tracked with satellite positioning receivers fixed to the ground, with sensitive tiltmeters that detect tiny changes in slope, and with radar images taken from orbit, which allow scientists to map swelling across an entire region without setting foot on it. Satellite radar has proved especially valuable for remote volcanoes, some of which were found to be inflating even though no one had suspected they were active.

D. Gases offer further clues. As magma rises, falling pressure allows dissolved gases such as carbon dioxide and sulphur dioxide to escape, much as bubbles appear when a bottle of fizzy drink is opened. Changes in the quantity of these gases, or in the ratio between them, can reveal that fresh magma has arrived from depth. More recently, researchers have begun literally to listen to volcanoes using microphones that record infrasound, sound at frequencies too low for the human ear. Because infrasound can travel hundreds or even thousands of kilometres through the atmosphere, a few well-placed stations can detect explosions at volcanoes where no local instruments exist.

E. When these methods are combined, the results can be impressive. In 1991, warnings based on monitoring at Mount Pinatubo in the Philippines allowed authorities to evacuate tens of thousands of people before one of the largest eruptions of the twentieth century. However, such successes depend on knowing what is normal for a particular volcano, and that requires years of continuous observation. A large proportion of the world's potentially active volcanoes have no permanent instruments at all, often because they are in poorer countries or difficult terrain. To address this, engineer Dmitri Saavedra has developed solar-powered seismic sensors that cost a fraction of conventional equipment; he argues that a dense network of cheap instruments may be more useful than a few expensive ones.

F. The deeper difficulty is that unrest does not always end in an eruption. Magma may rise, stall and cool underground, producing all the warning signs without ever reaching the surface. In a study of historical records from volcanoes on several continents, geophysicist Ngaire Holcombe found that more than half of the well-documented episodes of unrest she examined ended without any eruption. She also found that the length of unrest varied enormously: some volcanoes erupted within days of the first signs, while others remained restless for over a decade before finally erupting or falling quiet. In Holcombe's view, this means that no single threshold, such as a certain number of earthquakes per day, can be applied to every volcano.

G. For this reason, many observatories now express forecasts as probabilities rather than firm predictions, and they use alert levels, often shown as colours, to communicate changing risk. This approach is more honest, but it creates its own problems. Officials must decide whether to order evacuations that disrupt lives and businesses, knowing that the volcano may do nothing. Repeated false alarms can lead residents to ignore future warnings, while a delayed decision can be fatal. Some observatories now hold regular public meetings in nearby villages to explain what the alert levels mean. Volcanologists increasingly argue that their task is not only to read the instruments but also to build trust with the communities who must act on what those instruments reveal.

### Секция 2: The True Cost of Cheap Clothes

A. For most of the twentieth century, the clothing trade ran to a slow and predictable calendar. Collections were planned as much as a year ahead, manufactured in large volumes and delivered to shops twice a year, once for spring and summer and once for autumn and winter. Retailers had to guess what customers would want long before anyone wore it, and when they guessed wrongly, unsold stock was cleared through end-of-season sales. Fast fashion overturned this arrangement. Its leading companies now introduce new lines every week, and some online sellers list thousands of new designs a day. As a result, clothing has become much cheaper relative to most other goods, while the number of garments the average shopper buys has risen sharply.

B. The method behind this speed is less about sewing faster than about deciding later. A typical cycle begins with observation: design teams study catwalk shows, street style and social media, looking for colours and shapes that are attracting attention. Rather than inventing styles of their own, they adapt existing ones, producing simplified versions that can be made cheaply. A small initial batch, sometimes only a few hundred pieces, is then manufactured and sent to selected stores or listed online. After that, sales figures are monitored hourly; items that sell quickly are reordered within days, while those that fail are withdrawn before much money has been lost. Because the company commits to large quantities only once demand has been demonstrated, it largely escapes the heavy discounting that once ate into retailers' profits.

C. Speed of this kind depends on geography as much as on information. Many fast-fashion firms keep part of their production close to their main markets. For European retailers, that often means factories in Turkey, Morocco or Eastern Europe, where wages are higher than in South Asia but finished goods can arrive by lorry within a week instead of spending a month at sea. Basic items such as plain T-shirts, socks and underwear, for which demand changes little from year to year, are still made in large volumes in countries such as Bangladesh and Vietnam. Nikhil Barrantes, a supply-chain consultant, describes this as a 'two-speed system': companies pay extra for flexibility on trend-led products and squeeze costs on everything else.

D. The pressure to be both cheap and quick does not disappear; it is passed down the chain to factories. Brands typically negotiate a fixed price per garment and may alter quantities or designs at short notice, and suppliers who fear losing contracts rarely refuse. Valeria Ostrowski, a labour economist, interviewed around 300 garment workers in two exporting countries and concluded that last-minute changes to orders were more closely linked to unpaid overtime than the low prices paid per item. In her view, the problem lies as much in the buying practices of brands as in the wages set by factory owners. Safety is a further concern. In 2013, the collapse of a factory building near Dhaka, Bangladesh, killed more than a thousand garment workers and led to a legally binding agreement under which many international brands committed to supporting independent safety inspections.

E. The environmental bill is just as large. Growing cotton requires vast quantities of water, and dyeing and finishing fabric release chemicals that, where regulation is weak, often end up in rivers. Polyester, now the most widely used fibre, is made from oil and sheds tiny plastic fibres whenever it is washed. Perhaps the most striking change, however, concerns how clothes are used rather than how they are made. Research suggests that the average garment is now worn considerably fewer times before being thrown away than at the start of this century, and only a tiny fraction of discarded clothing is recycled into new garments. Much of the rest is buried or burned, while bales of second-hand clothing are shipped to markets in countries such as Ghana and Kenya, where traders report that a growing share is too worn or poorly made to sell.

F. Economists have a name for costs of this kind: externalities. These are costs that fall on people outside a transaction, such as communities living downstream of a dye works or workers whose health suffers, and they are not reflected in the price the shopper pays. When harmful effects are left out of prices, markets tend to produce more of a product than society would choose if it saw the full picture. One response is extended producer responsibility, under which companies contribute to the cost of collecting and recycling what they sell. France has applied such a scheme to clothing since 2007, and the European Union is moving towards similar rules.

G. Whether shoppers will change their habits is less certain. Resale and rental services have grown quickly, and many consumers tell surveys that sustainability matters to them. Yet a study by retail analyst Joana Hatfield, which tracked the purchases of 2,000 online customers, found that those who regularly sold clothes through resale apps bought more new items, not fewer, apparently because the prospect of reselling made each purchase feel less costly. If her findings hold, good intentions alone may not slow the cycle; what may matter more is whether prices begin to reflect the true costs of production.

### Секция 3: Starting Late: The Case for Adult Musicians

A. Ask a room of adults whether they wish they had learned to play an instrument, and a surprising number of hands will go up. Ask them why they do not start now, and the answer is usually some version of the same idea: the chance has passed. Music, according to this view, is a skill that must be planted in childhood, when the brain is supposedly at its most receptive, and anyone who begins in middle age is condemned to clumsy mediocrity. I think this belief is largely wrong, and that it discourages a great many people from an activity that could enrich their lives. Adults can learn an instrument, and most who want to should try, provided they understand what they are, and are not, signing up for.

B. The case for an early start is not invented. Researchers have found that people who began serious training in early childhood are more likely to have absolute pitch, the rare ability to name a note without hearing a reference, and brain-imaging studies have reported structural differences in those who trained from a young age. Yet these findings are narrower than they are often made to sound. Absolute pitch is impressive, but plenty of celebrated professionals manage without it, and it is of almost no consequence to someone whose ambition is to accompany friends singing folk songs. Brain differences, meanwhile, tell us that early training leaves a mark; they do not tell us that later training leaves none.

C. Signe Valdés, a cognitive psychologist, followed 140 beginners over two years: eighty adults aged between 35 and 68, and sixty children aged 8 to 11, all taking weekly piano lessons. The adults' advantage appeared early and in a place she had not expected. They grasped notation, rhythm and the logic of chords considerably faster than the children, apparently because they could bring years of general learning, including reading and mathematics, to bear on a new symbolic system. Where they fell behind was in the hands. Smooth, automatic finger movement came more slowly, and many adults reported the frustration of knowing exactly what a passage should sound like while being unable to produce it. By the end of the second year, however, the gap in physical fluency had narrowed noticeably, though it had not closed.

D. That frustration, according to music educator Kwabena Rautio, is the real danger for adult learners, more than any limitation of the body. Having interviewed several hundred people who had abandoned lessons, he concluded that most gave up not because progress stopped but because they judged it too harshly. Children tend to measure themselves against the child at the next music stand; adults, by contrast, measure themselves against the polished recordings they have listened to for decades, and inevitably find themselves wanting. Rautio's remedy is partly social. Beginners who learn in small groups, he argues, see others stumbling over the same passages, which makes their own mistakes seem ordinary rather than shameful.

E. Much of the public enthusiasm for adult music-making rests on a different promise: that it keeps the mind young. Here I believe the claims have run well ahead of the evidence. Lucinda Ferreyra, who studies ageing, surveyed members of community ukulele and recorder groups for retired people and found that they reported better mood and memory than other residents of the same towns. She is careful, though, to point out what her data cannot show. The players had chosen to join, and people who volunteer for a weekly group activity may already be healthier, more sociable and more optimistic than those who do not. Without following comparable people who were randomly assigned to lessons, she says, it is impossible to know whether the music produced the benefits or merely attracted those who already had them. Newspaper headlines announcing that the violin 'protects the brain' rarely include such caveats.

F. More practical guidance comes from Dong-hyun Albrecht, who examined how working adults organise their practice. Comparing learners in his study who had similar total practice time, he found that those who played for around twenty minutes on most days advanced more quickly than those who saved their effort for long sessions at the weekend. Albrecht also noticed that the choice of instrument mattered more than people expected. Learners who began on instruments that produce a pleasant tone almost immediately, such as the piano or ukulele, were more likely to persist than those who began on the violin, whose early squeaks can discourage even a determined beginner.

G. None of this means that a fifty-year-old beginner will become a concert soloist, and it would be dishonest to suggest otherwise. But that was never the right measure. The reasons for an adult to learn an instrument are the pleasure of making something with one's own hands, the satisfaction of slow improvement, and the company of others doing the same. Those who start for these reasons, rather than in the hope of a cognitive cure, are the ones most likely to find the effort repaid. The door, in other words, is not closed; it simply opens onto a different room from the one children enter.

- ru: Абзац E: Ferreyra предупреждает, что участники её опроса сами решили присоединиться, поэтому они, возможно, были здоровее и счастливее ещё до начала занятий.
- uk: Абзац E: Ferreyra застерігає, що учасники її опитування самі вирішили приєднатися, тому вони, можливо, були здоровішими й щасливішими ще до початку занять.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: C ✓; GPT-5.4: C ✓; Grok: C ✓.

**35.** Short practice sessions on most days led to faster progress than long sessions done less often.

- en: Paragraph F reports Albrecht's finding that about twenty minutes on most days beat long weekend sessions.

- uk: В абзаці F наводиться висновок Albrecht: близько двадцяти хвилин у більшість днів дають кращий результат, ніж довгі заняття у вихідні.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**37.** What point does the writer make about brain-imaging studies of early musical training?

- A. They reveal an effect of early training without ruling out later change.
- B. They show that most celebrated professionals began their training as adults.
- C. They are less dependable than research on absolute pitch in young children.
- D. They prove that adult brains can no longer be altered by musical training.

- Ключ: **A (They reveal an effect of early training without ruling out later change.)**
- Доказательство: «they do not tell us that later training leaves none»
- en: In paragraph B the writer says brain differences show early training 'leaves a mark' but do not show that later training has no effect. The passage never says adult brains cannot change, and it does not compare how reliable the two kinds of research are.
- ru: В абзаце B автор говорит, что различия в мозге показывают: раннее обучение «leaves a mark», — но не доказывают, что более позднее обучение не даёт эффекта. В тексте нигде не сказано, что мозг взрослых не может меняться, и надёжность двух видов исследований не сравнивается.
- uk: В абзаці B автор каже, що відмінності в мозку показують: раннє навчання «leaves a mark», — але не доводять, що пізніше навчання не дає ефекту. У тексті ніде не сказано, що мозок дорослих не може змінюватися, і надійність двох видів досліджень не порівнюється.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: A ✓; GPT-5.4: A ✓; Grok: A ✓.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**38.** According to Valdés, why did the adults in her study learn notation more quickly?

- A. They had listened to more recordings of piano music for decades.
- B. They could apply earlier learning to an unfamiliar set of symbols.

- Ключ: **B (They could apply earlier learning to an unfamiliar set of symbols.)**

- uk: В абзаці C пояснюється, що дорослі застосовували до нової системи музичних символів багаторічний досвід загального навчання, наприклад читання й математики. Щотижневі уроки мали всі початківці, а записи згадує Rautio, причому зовсім з іншого приводу.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: A ✓; GPT-5.4: A ✓; Grok: B ✓.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**39.** How, according to Rautio, do adult learners differ from children?

- A. Adults give up sooner because of the physical limits of their bodies.
- B. Adults make more mistakes than children during their first lessons.
- C. Adults judge their playing against experts, not fellow learners.
- D. Adults prefer studying alone to studying in small groups of beginners.

- Ключ: **C (Adults judge their playing against experts, not fellow learners.)**
- Доказательство: «adults, by contrast, measure themselves against the polished recordings they have listened to for decades»
- en: In paragraph D Rautio says children compare themselves with the child next to them, while adults compare themselves with polished recordings. He says frustration matters more than any limitation of the body, so the physical option is wrong.
- ru: В абзаце D Rautio говорит, что дети сравнивают себя с ребёнком, сидящим рядом, а взрослые — с отшлифованными записями. По его словам, разочарование важнее любых физических ограничений, поэтому вариант о физических причинах неверен.
- uk: В абзаці D Rautio каже, що діти порівнюють себе з дитиною поруч, а дорослі — з відшліфованими записами. За його словами, розчарування важить більше, ніж будь-які фізичні обмеження, тому варіант про фізичні причини хибний.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: A ✓; GPT-5.4: A ✓; Grok: C ✓.

#### multiple_choice: Choose the correct letter, A, B, C or D.

**40.** What does Ferreyra say would be needed to show that music caused the benefits she observed?

- A. Comparing the players of several different kinds of instrument.
- B. Surveying players in a much wider range of towns and regions.
- C. Following the same ukulele and recorder groups for many more years.
- D. Tracking similar people placed in lessons purely by chance.

- Ключ: **D (Tracking similar people placed in lessons purely by chance.)**
- Доказательство: «Without following comparable people who were randomly assigned to lessons, she says, it is impossible to know»
- en: Paragraph E: Ferreyra says that without comparable people randomly assigned to lessons, nobody can know whether the music caused the benefits. More towns, longer study of the same groups or different instruments would not solve the problem that the players chose to join.
- ru: Абзац E: Ferreyra говорит, что без сопоставимых людей, которых случайным образом направили бы на занятия, никто не может знать, действительно ли польза вызвана музыкой. Ни больше городов, ни более долгое наблюдение за теми же группами, ни другие инструменты не решили бы главную проблему: участники сами решили присоединиться.
- uk: Абзац E: Ferreyra каже, що без порівнянних людей, яких випадково розподілили б на заняття, ніхто не може знати, чи справді користь спричинила саме музика. Ні більше міст, ні довше спостереження за тими самими групами, ні інші інструменти не розв'язали б головну проблему: учасники самі вирішили приєднатися.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: A ✓; GPT-5.4: A ✓; Grok: D ✓.

## Тест academic-10

Проверка кодом: без замечаний. Слов в текстах: 2586. Полоса при 30/40: {"low":7,"high":7}.

### Секция 1: The Return of the Wolf

A. For much of the twentieth century, the grey wolf was little more than a memory across most of western and central Europe. Centuries of hunting, often encouraged by bounties paid to anyone who produced a dead animal, combined with poisoning and the clearing of forests to push the species out of one country after another. By the 1960s, wolves survived mainly in scattered refuges: the mountains of the Iberian Peninsula, the Apennines of Italy, the Balkans and the great forests of eastern Europe. Today, by contrast, wolves breed in most mainland European countries, and recent estimates put the continent's population at well over ten thousand animals. Few large carnivores anywhere have staged such a comeback, and remarkably, it has happened not in wilderness but in one of the most densely populated and intensively farmed regions on Earth.

B. The first reason for this recovery is legal. For most of history, killing a wolf was not merely permitted but regarded as a public service. That attitude began to change in the 1970s. Italy gave the wolf legal protection in 1971, at a time when perhaps only 100 animals remained in the Apennines. In 1979 the Bern Convention, a European agreement on the conservation of wildlife and natural habitats, listed the wolf as strictly protected, and later European Union law reinforced this status. Some countries, Spain among them, kept exceptions that allowed limited hunting in particular regions, but across much of the continent the threat of being shot was sharply reduced for the first time in centuries.

C. Changes in the countryside mattered just as much. During the second half of the twentieth century, millions of rural Europeans left the land for jobs in towns and cities. Hillside fields that had been grazed or ploughed for generations were abandoned and slowly returned to scrub and woodland. This process gave wolves both cover and, more importantly, food. Populations of red deer, roe deer and wild boar expanded dramatically, partly because there were fewer people to hunt them and partly because the new forests provided ideal shelter. A predator returning to these landscapes found a far richer supply of wild prey than its ancestors had known a century earlier.

D. The third factor is the wolf itself. Young wolves usually leave the pack into which they were born at between one and two years old, setting off in search of a mate and a territory of their own. Some travel astonishing distances. Wolves from Italy crossed into the French Alps in the early 1990s, and in 2000 a pair that had wandered west from Poland raised cubs in eastern Germany, the first known breeding there for well over a century. Ilka Brunner, an ecologist who followed forty young wolves fitted with satellite collars, found that some covered more than 800 kilometres before settling and that most crossed roads and farmland mainly at night, when people were indoors. 'They are not creatures of the wilderness,' she says. 'They are adaptable animals that were kept out of our landscapes by guns, not by any lack of suitable habitat.'

E. Not everyone welcomes their return. For sheep and goat farmers, particularly in areas where wolves had been absent for generations, the arrival of a pack can be devastating. In regions where wolves never disappeared, such as central Italy, shepherds kept up traditions of guarding flocks with dogs and bringing animals into enclosures at night. In places like the French Alps or the plains of northern Germany, by contrast, such practices had largely been forgotten, and flocks often grazed unattended for weeks. Aurelio Mancuso, who keeps around 400 sheep in the Abruzzo mountains, believes the real problem is less the wolf than the loss of old skills. 'My grandfather never left his sheep alone,' he says. 'Farmers elsewhere are now having to relearn what we never forgot.' Many governments pay compensation for animals that are killed, but farmers frequently complain that payments arrive late and fail to cover the stress, the extra work or the loss of valuable breeding stock.

F. Increasingly, therefore, governments are investing in prevention rather than payment. Electric fencing and specially bred guardian dogs, often from traditional breeds such as Italy's Maremma sheepdog, are now subsidised in several countries. Their effectiveness, however, depends heavily on how they are used. Ngozi Abara, a social scientist who interviewed more than 200 livestock owners, found that farmers who received training as well as equipment reported far fewer losses than those who were simply handed fences. She also discovered that attitudes towards wolves were shaped less by the number of animals a farmer had lost than by a sense that decisions were being taken by distant officials. Pressure from rural communities has already led to moves to lower the wolf's protected status and permit more culling. Whether Europe can share its crowded landscapes with a large predator may ultimately depend less on biology than on whether the people who bear the costs feel they are being heard.

#### matching_information: The reading passage has six paragraphs, A–F. Which paragraph contains the following information? Write the correct letter, A–F. You may use any letter more than once.

- **A** A
- **B** B
- **C** C
- **D** D
- **E** E
- **F** F

**1.** a reference to rewards once offered for killing wolves

- Ключ: **A (A)**
- Доказательство: «bounties paid to anyone who produced a dead animal»
- en: Paragraph A says that hunting was encouraged by bounties, which are payments or rewards for each dead wolf. Paragraph B calls killing wolves a 'public service' but does not mention any payment.

- uk: В абзаці A сказано, що полювання заохочували за допомогою "bounties" — виплат або винагород за кожного вбитого вовка. В абзаці B вбивство вовків названо "public service", але про жодну оплату там не йдеться.
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: A ✓; GPT-5.4: A ✓; Grok: A ✓.

**2.** an example of a country that continued to permit some wolf hunting

- Ключ: **B (B)**
- Доказательство: «Spain among them, kept exceptions that allowed limited hunting in particular regions»
- en: Paragraph B names Spain as a country that kept exceptions allowing limited hunting, even after the wolf became protected.
- ru: В абзаце B Испания названа страной, которая сохранила исключения, разрешающие ограниченную охоту, даже после того как волк получил охранный статус.
- uk: В абзаці B Іспанію названо країною, яка зберегла винятки, що дозволяють обмежене полювання, навіть після того як вовк отримав охоронний статус.

**3.** a claim that wolves were excluded by human hostility rather than by unsuitable surroundings

- en: In Paragraph D, Ilka Brunner says wolves were kept out by guns (people killing them), not because the habitat was unsuitable.

**4.** a contrast between farming traditions in areas with and without a continuous wolf presence

- en: Paragraph E compares shepherds in central Italy, where wolves never disappeared, with farmers in the French Alps and northern Germany, where guarding practices had been forgotten.

### Секция 2: The Underrated Nose

A. For much of modern history, smell has been treated as the poor relation of the human senses. Philosophers ranked it below sight and hearing, regarding it as crude and animal-like, and nineteenth-century anatomists seemed to supply evidence for that prejudice. Comparing brains, they noticed that the olfactory bulb, the structure that first receives signals from the nose, occupies a far smaller share of the human brain than it does in dogs or rodents. From this they concluded that humans had traded a keen nose for a larger thinking cortex. The idea proved remarkably durable, passing from textbooks into popular belief, and only in recent decades has it been seriously challenged.

B. To understand why, it helps to know how smelling actually works. When we breathe in, molecules released by objects around us travel to a small patch of tissue high in the nasal cavity, roughly the size of a postage stamp. There, millions of sensory nerve cells carry receptors on hair-like projections bathed in mucus. Humans possess around 400 different types of working receptor, and each type responds to a range of molecules rather than to a single one. A given odour therefore activates a particular combination of receptors, rather like a chord played on a piano, and the brain learns to recognise the pattern. Because the number of possible combinations is enormous, a modest set of receptors can distinguish a vast number of smells. Unusually for nerve cells, these sensory cells are not permanent: they are replaced regularly throughout life, roughly every one to two months.

C. The route these signals take inside the brain is equally distinctive. Information from the eyes, ears and skin passes through a relay station called the thalamus before reaching the brain's outer layers. Smell signals, by contrast, travel from the olfactory bulb straight to regions involved in emotion, such as the amygdala, and to areas closely tied to memory. This short path may explain why a particular scent — a grandparent's kitchen, the polish on a school corridor — can bring back a vivid scene from childhood before we have even identified what we are smelling. In one study by the psychologist Folasade Wren, volunteers given smells as memory cues recalled events that were, on average, older and more emotionally charged than those prompted by written words or photographs. However, they rated these smell-cued memories as less clear in their details.

D. If the machinery is so sophisticated, why did the belief in a weak human nose persist for so long? Part of the answer lies in that original anatomical comparison. The neuroscientist Oskar Lindwall argues that judging the bulb against total brain size was misleading. When his team counted nerve cells instead of comparing proportions, they found that the human olfactory bulb contains roughly as many neurons as those of several mammals famed for their sense of smell. 'A small room in a large house is still a room,' Lindwall remarks. 'The human bulb looks modest only because everything around it grew.'

E. Behavioural evidence points in the same direction. Malika Duchesne, who studies sensory perception, tested how little of certain substances needed to be present in the air before people could notice them. For a handful of compounds, including one that gives bananas their scent, her volunteers responded at concentrations as low as those previously recorded for dogs and mice. Duchesne is careful not to overstate the result: dogs remain far better at most tasks, particularly at following a scent trail across the ground. Her point is rather that human ability varies greatly from one substance to another, so sweeping claims that humans smell badly conceal a more complicated picture.

F. A further reason for the underestimation is linguistic. Most European languages have few words reserved for smells; speakers tend to describe an odour by naming its source, as in 'it smells like smoke', rather than with abstract terms comparable to 'red' or 'loud'. The linguist Hyun-woo Baek suggests that this poverty of vocabulary has been mistaken for a poverty of perception. In his experiments, adults who spent several weeks learning invented names for twenty unfamiliar odours became noticeably better at telling similar smells apart, while a control group who simply sniffed the same samples without labels improved only slightly. For Baek, the trouble many people have in identifying smells reflects a lack of practice and vocabulary rather than any weakness in the nose itself.

G. Interest in smell has grown sharply in recent years, partly because so many people temporarily lost the sense during the COVID-19 pandemic. Their experiences revealed how much it contributes to everyday life. Without it, food becomes dull, since much of what we call taste is in fact smell, as odours rise to the nose from the back of the mouth while we chew. Many sufferers also reported feeling isolated or low. Smell training, which involves sniffing a set of strong odours twice a day over several months, is now seen as a promising aid to recovery, although its effectiveness is still being investigated. Far from being a leftover from our animal past, the human nose appears to be a capable and underused instrument.

### Секция 3: The Case Against the Worksheet

A. Every weekday evening in households around the world, a familiar scene unfolds: a seven-year-old at the kitchen table, a worksheet of sums in front of her, and a tired parent trying to remember how long division was taught. Homework for young children has become so ordinary that it is rarely questioned. Schools set it partly because parents expect it, and parents expect it partly because schools set it. Yet when we ask what this nightly ritual actually achieves for pupils under the age of eleven, the answer is far less convincing than its popularity suggests. My own view is that most formal homework in primary school should be abolished, and replaced with something simpler and more valuable.

B. Start with the evidence. Large reviews of research, which pool the results of many separate studies, have repeatedly found that the link between homework and academic achievement is clearest among older teenagers and weak, or barely detectable, among younger pupils. This is not surprising. A fifteen-year-old preparing for examinations can consolidate what was taught in class and practise independently. A six-year-old, by contrast, often lacks the concentration and the organisational skills to work alone, so the task either goes unfinished or is quietly completed by an adult. In neither case does the child learn much.

C. Defenders of homework argue that its purpose in the early years is not to raise test scores but to build habits. Children who learn to sit down and finish a task at home, the argument runs, will be better prepared for the heavier demands of secondary school. This deserves to be taken seriously, but it rests on an assumption that has rarely been tested. Ingeborg Lunde, an education researcher who followed 400 pupils from the age of seven to fourteen, found no difference in the study routines of teenagers who had received regular homework in primary school and those who had received almost none. Her study is a single one, and its sample was drawn from just two towns, so it should not be treated as the final word. Still, it suggests that good habits may be learned when they are needed, rather than drilled years in advance.

D. There are also costs that rarely appear in the debate. Homework takes time from activities that matter enormously at this age: unstructured play, physical exercise, conversation with family and, not least, sleep. It can also become a source of conflict. Femi Adewale, the head of a primary school that stopped setting worksheets five years ago, says that the most common complaint he used to hear from parents was not that tasks were too hard but that evenings had turned into arguments. Since the change, he reports, evenings at home have become calmer, although he admits he has no firm data on pupils' results. Moreover, homework tends to widen gaps between children. A pupil whose parents have the time, confidence and education to help will return with polished work; a pupil whose parents work late shifts or struggle with the language of instruction may return with nothing, and feel ashamed.

E. None of this means that learning should stop at the school gate. The home activity that researchers most consistently link with progress at this age is reading, and in particular reading for pleasure. Children who read widely at home tend to develop richer vocabularies and better comprehension, and the benefit appears to grow over time. Crucially, reading does not require a parent to be an expert. A child can read alone, read aloud to a younger sibling, or listen to a story at bedtime. At Adewale's school, the worksheets were replaced by a simple expectation: twenty minutes of reading each evening, with the choice of book left entirely to the child.

F. Mariana Sotelo, a child psychologist, adds a further point. In her view, the problem with much primary homework is that it is designed around what is easy to mark rather than what is useful to learn. Worksheets can be checked quickly, but they rarely invite curiosity. She suggests that, where schools feel they must set something, it should be practical and open-ended — measuring ingredients for a recipe, for instance, or interviewing a grandparent about their childhood. Such tasks connect classroom learning to everyday life and give families something to share rather than something to fight over.

G. Critics will say that abolishing homework sends the wrong message, implying that education is unimportant. I would argue the opposite. By protecting children's evenings, schools signal that rest, play and family life are part of a good education, not distractions from it. Some parents, it is true, value homework as a window into what their child is studying; a short weekly note from the teacher could serve that purpose just as well. Schools also free teachers from the considerable burden of setting and checking tasks that, for this age group, do little good, and that time is better spent on lessons themselves.

H. The question, then, is not whether young children should learn at home, but how. A nightly book, chosen freely and enjoyed without pressure, will do more for most seven-year-olds than any number of photocopied sums. Schools that have the courage to stop setting worksheets are not lowering their standards; they are aligning their practice with what the evidence actually shows.

- uk: В абзаці B сказано, що, за даними оглядів, зв'язок між домашніми завданнями й успішністю найчіткіше видно у старших підлітків. Це і є тісніший зв'язок з успішністю у старших учнів.

**37.** A typical six-year-old does not yet have the concentration or the ______ required to work without help.

- uk: В абзаці B сказано, що шестирічній дитині часто бракує зосередженості ("concentration") та організаційних навичок ("organisational skills"), щоб працювати самостійно; "without help" — це перефразоване "alone".
- Решатели (первый проход) и контролёр: Sonnet: organisational skills ✓; GPT-5.4: organisational skills ✓; Grok: organisational skills ✓.

- uk: В абзаці C сказано, що її вибірку взяли лише з двох міст ("two towns") — це одна з причин, чому дослідження не варто вважати останнім словом.
